# build-data log

## Session 109, 2026-11-25: a seed-spread guard on the Y-6 chooser's short lean (pre-registered; outcome below)

Why: session 108's chooser over {1, ..., 5} years (chooser5) is the best rule tested on all 18 city-years (pooled q0.9 regret 1.62 days against the G5 oracle, worst cell 6.03), and its whole remaining cost is that it takes the 1- and 2-year cuts in SF 2017 to 2021, where builds are long. Session 108's next step (1): a guard that takes a shorter cut only when it wins the target's Y-6 score by more than the score's own seed noise, fixed before scoring. Each saved fit holds its Y-6 (k6) loss per seed, so the guard needs no new model run. The practical question: can a planning team that uses the chooser tell from its own three seeds when the chooser's preference for a short cut is noise, and does acting on that keep Austin's gain while removing SF's cost?
Design (fixed before any per-seed k6 value was looked at): `src/guard5.py`, unit-tested on synthetic cells before any run (`tests/test_guard5.py`, 4 tests). Means and test losses come from session 108's scored cells (`results/rounds_score_floor5_i2c_floor5.json`); per-seed k6 from the raw fit files of sessions 101 to 108, the first whose k6 and test means match the scored cell within 1e-6. Rules, all scored against the G5 oracle: **spread** (primary): v* = chooser5's pick; s = max minus min of v*'s three per-seed k6 losses; pick the longest cut whose mean k6 is within s of v*'s (a one-spread rule after cross-validation's one-standard-error rule, leaning to the cut that keeps more source data); **se**: the same with s = sd/sqrt(3); **regime**: take the 1-year cut only if its k6 is below the best of {2, ..., 5} by more than its own seed spread, else chooser4's pick. A post-hoc sweep of the spread rule's margin (k times s, k = 0 to 10) is reported, not tested.
Already seen (disclosed): every cell's mean test and k6 losses and the picks of chooser5 and chooser4 (sessions 103 to 108), so the guard's form was chosen knowing that the chooser leans short in SF; no per-seed k6 value, spread or gap between seeds has been printed or read for this registration. No rule parameter is fitted; the margin is the winner's own seed spread.
Predictions (regret = a rule's test q0.9 minus the G5 oracle's, days, mean over cells):
- (D0, check) Every one of the 90 cell-cuts finds a raw fit whose k6 and test means match the scored cell within 1e-6. Prior 85%.
- (D1, primary) Pooled over 18 cells, spread's mean regret is below chooser5's 1.62. Prior 55%.
- (D2) Over the 12 SF/Seattle cells, spread's mean regret is below chooser5's 2.28. Prior 60%.
- (D3) Over the 6 Austin cells, spread's mean regret is at most 1.00 (chooser5 0.32). Prior 60%.
- (D4) spread's worst cell is no worse than chooser5's 6.03. Prior 55%.
- (D5) spread changes chooser5's pick in at least 4 of 18 cells. Prior 50%.
- (D6) Pooled, regime's mean regret is below chooser5's. Prior 45%.
- (D7) Pooled, se's mean regret is below chooser5's. Prior 45%.
Each refuted if it fails. Risks named in advance: three seeds give a noisy spread (a range of three draws), so the margin itself is noisy; seeds vary the model, not the permits, so the spread understates the Y-6 cohort's sampling noise; the cells are in-sample for the guard's motivation, so a gain here is weaker evidence than one on a fresh city.

## Session 108, 2026-11-19: with the 1-year cut on the menu the chooser leaves 1.62 days of pooled q0.9 regret against 2.38 for the 2- to 5-year chooser (G5 oracle, 18 city-years), all of it from Austin; outside Austin the 1-year cut is barely worse than the 2-year one (SF/Seattle mean gap 0.65 days, against 3.32 from 2 to 3 years), the chooser takes it in SF 2017 to 2021 and gives back 0.21 days per SF/Seattle cell; C0, C2, C3, C6 held, C1, C4, C5 refuted

Why: session 107 found that for Austin, whose builds are shorter than its sources', the borrowed model does best with no open source permits at all (the 1-year cut, mean q0.9 59.99 against 62.67 at 2 years), and that the Y-6 chooser ranks nc1 lowest in all 6 Austin years. nc1 was not fitted for SF or Seattle, so a chooser with it on the menu could not be scored pooled. Session 107's next step (1). The practical question: if a planning team puts "train on completed source builds only" on the chooser's menu, does the chooser keep it for the short-build city and leave it alone for the long-build ones, or does its lean to short cuts (SF 2017 to 2020 in session 107) make it take the 1-year cut where it costs days?
Design (fixed before any model run): unchanged `rounds.py` (`cpin_es3_nc1`, 365 days, added in session 107; `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`, `--score --keep-open --status-dir`), cascade2, 3 seeds; variant `cpin_es3_nc1` for H = sf, seattle (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin) and Y = 2017 to 2022 (12 fits); fresh pull 2026-11-19; one process per held city and pair of years at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_floor5_i2c.json`, scored by `src/tfol_boot.py --floor5` (`floor5_summary`, written and unit-tested in this commit before any fit, `tests/test_tfol_floor5.py`). Everything else comes from session 107's scored cells (`results/rounds_score_floor_i2c_floor.json`): nc2 to nc5 for all 18 cells, nc1 for Austin. G5 = {nc1, nc2, nc3, nc4, nc5}; oracle = best G5 member on the test year; chooser5 = lowest k6_loss over G5, chooser4 = the same over G4 = {nc2, ..., nc5}, both scored against the G5 oracle; regret = a rule minus the G5 oracle in a cell.
Already seen (disclosed): the Austin half of every pooled figure is fixed by session 107's numbers. Against the G5 oracle chooser5 picks nc1 in all 6 Austin cells (total regret 1.93 days, all in 2020) and chooser4 picks nc2 (total 17.99), so chooser5 starts the pooled comparison 16.06 days ahead; C3 holds unless chooser5's total regret over the 12 SF/Seattle cells exceeds chooser4's by 16.06 days or more. C4 scores the unseen half alone. No nc1 number for SF or Seattle has been computed in any session.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (C0, check) The saved rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001 for all 12 fits, and every nc1 fit has `n_train_open` = `n_es_open` = 0 (at 1 year every source permit open at D is older than the cut, as in Austin). Prior 90%.
- (C1) nc1 is above nc2 in at least 10 of the 12 SF/Seattle cells. Prior 80%.
- (C2) chooser5 picks nc1 in at least 4 of the 12 SF/Seattle cells (the lean to short cuts carries down another step in SF 2017 to 2020). Prior 55%.
- (C3, primary) Pooled over the 18 cells, chooser5's mean regret is below chooser4's: adding the 1-year cut to the menu helps overall. Prior 55%.
- (C4) Over the 12 SF/Seattle cells, chooser5's mean regret is no more than chooser4's (the 1-year cut costs nothing where builds are long). Prior 40%.
- (C5) Over the 12 SF/Seattle cells, the mean of nc1 minus nc2 exceeds the mean of nc2 minus nc3 (5.05 minus 1.73 = 3.32 days in session 107): each year of staleness taken away costs more than the last. Prior 55%.
- (C6) Pooled over the 18 cells, nc3 has the lowest mean regret of G5's five fixed cuts. Prior 70%.
Each refuted if it fails. Risks named in advance: nc1 trains on completed source builds only, so for a long-build target it may underpredict the tail badly, and the Y-6 cohort (permits issued six years before D and completed by D) is itself biased toward short builds, which is the mechanism behind the chooser's lean; the nc2 to nc5 cells come from a pull a few hours older (session 107, 14:07 UTC); 3 seeds; q0.9 only.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 94bc8550b, before any model run (draft PR #17757). Pull 2026-11-19 18:40 to 18:49 UTC: SF 1,298,572 rows (750,934 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,386,174 (2,187,174), the same counts as session 107's pull five hours earlier; status pull in the same window. Fits on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 18:50 to 19:23 UTC, four processes (SF 2017 to 2019, SF 2020 to 2022, Seattle 2017 to 2019, Seattle 2020 to 2022) at 1 thread; Seattle 2018 and 2019 were moved to their own processes after Seattle 2017's three seeds took 400 to 515 s each (same pull, seeds and code, so the same fits), and the first Seattle process was stopped once 2017 was saved. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_floor5_i2c.json` (12 fits), `results/rounds_score_floor5_i2c_floor5.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --floor5`).
- Test q0.9 of nc1 per cell (days; nc2, nc3, nc4, nc5 from session 107 after it): SF 2017 83.32 (82.50, 81.80, 83.17, 90.66), 2018 89.34 (89.38, 86.19, 85.46, 89.13), 2019 87.49 (88.71, 83.31, 81.75, 82.17), 2020 84.00 (83.57, 79.05, 78.12, 77.97), 2021 73.33 (73.22, 70.79, 71.33, 72.35), 2022 59.78 (59.35, 58.16, 59.04, 61.21); Seattle 2017 81.60 (80.80, 80.34, 79.91, 81.73), 2018 93.78 (92.73, 89.68, 88.48, 88.60), 2019 88.42 (88.73, 82.25, 80.30, 80.79), 2020 109.75 (107.14, 99.75, 97.25, 95.31), 2021 96.69 (95.00, 91.07, 87.82, 85.61), 2022 76.32 (74.97, 73.86, 72.22, 69.91).
- (C0, check) Held: the saved rows reproduce the JSON means for all 12 fits (largest difference 7.6e-6), and every nc1 fit has `n_train_open` = `n_es_open` = 0.
- (C1) Refuted: nc1 is above nc2 in 9 of 12 SF/Seattle cells, not 10; it is below in SF 2018 (by 0.04), SF 2019 (1.22) and Seattle 2019 (0.31).
- (C2) Held: chooser5 picks nc1 in 5 of 12 SF/Seattle cells (SF 2017 to 2021); in SF 2017 to 2020 the Y-6 score ranks the five cuts by length, as it ranked the four in session 107.
- (C3, primary) Held: pooled over 18 cells chooser5 leaves 1.62 days of mean regret against the G5 oracle, chooser4 2.38. The whole gain is Austin's (0.32 against 3.00 per cell), fixed before this session's fits as disclosed.
- (C4) Refuted: over the 12 SF/Seattle cells chooser5 leaves 2.28 days, chooser4 2.06. The 1-year cut costs 0.21 days per cell where builds are long: +0.82, -0.04, -1.22, +0.43 in SF 2017 to 2020 (nc1 in place of nc2) and +2.54 in SF 2021 (nc1 in place of nc3); the other 7 picks are unchanged.
- (C5) Refuted: in SF/Seattle the mean of nc1 minus nc2 is 0.65 days, far below the mean of nc2 minus nc3 (3.32). Taking the second-last year of staleness away costs little; the cost is in going from 3 years to 2.
- (C6) Held: pooled over 18 cells nc3 has the lowest mean regret of the five fixed cuts (3.27; nc1 3.91, nc2 4.37, nc4 6.88, nc5 9.26).
- Post hoc (not pre-registered): worst cell over the 18 against the G5 oracle: chooser5 6.03 (SF 2020), chooser4 7.33 (Austin 2019), nc3 10.76, nc1 14.44, nc2 11.83. The 5 oracle wins of nc1 are all Austin; the 1-year cut is never the best cut for SF or Seattle (best by cell there: nc3 3, nc4 5, nc5 4).
- What this says: for SF and Seattle as target the 1- and 2-year cuts are nearly the same rule (both drop almost every open source permit; mean 85.32 against 84.67 days), so the menu really has two regimes: completed source builds only, and a 3- to 5-year allowance. Austin wants the first, SF and Seattle the second, and the Y-6 chooser finds Austin's every time but takes the first regime in SF 2017 to 2021, where it costs 1.5 to 6.0 days a cell against the G5 oracle. The chooser over {1, ..., 5} is the best rule tested so far on all 18 city-years, both in mean regret (1.62) and in worst cell (6.03); its remaining cost is that short lean in SF.
- Limits: two of three targets are long-build cities and the third is the only short-build one, so the Austin gain rests on one city; 3 seeds; q0.9 only; nc2 to nc5 come from a pull five hours older with the same row counts; the G5 oracle changes the regret baseline, so the chooser4 figure here (2.38) is not session 107's 1.38 (against G4).
Next: (1) a guard on the short lean, fixed before scoring and tested on a fresh split (e.g. a regime chooser: take {completed only} only when it wins Y-6 by more than the per-seed spread of Y-6 losses, which the rows files already hold); (2) a fourth target city, short-build if one has a completion field and an open licence, to test whether Austin's regime is a pattern or one city; (3) q0.5 and q0.1 for the two regimes.

## Session 107, 2026-11-19: Austin's floor is below 2 years, and a 2-year cut on the chooser's menu helps it: the 2-year cut beats the 3-year cut in all 6 Austin city-years (mean q0.9 62.67 against 66.00 days) and loses in all 12 SF and Seattle ones; the 1-year cut, which keeps no open source permit at all, beats the 2-year cut in 5 of 6 Austin years (59.99); pooled over 18 city-years the Y-6 chooser over {2, 3, 4, 5} leaves 1.38 days of regret against 1.72 for the chooser over {3, 4, 5} and 2.27 for the best fixed cut (3 years); B1, B2, B6 held, B0, B3, B4, B5 refuted

Why: session 106 found the 3-year cut is the grid oracle in all 6 Austin cells, the floor of the {3, 4, 5}-year grid, and session 92's sweep (reported, not tested) had a 2-year cut beating it in Austin 2021 and 2022 by 1.95 and 2.78 days. Session 106's next step (1): extend the grid down to 2 years (and 1 for Austin) and score the chooser over {2, 3, 4, 5}, pre-registered over the 18 cells. The question for a practitioner: does the Y-6 chooser, which ranked the cuts correctly in every Austin year, still help once a cut shorter than every city's builds is on the menu, or does its known lean toward short cuts (session 103: k6 ranks the cuts by length in all six SF cells) make it pick the 2-year cut where it costs days?
Design (fixed before any model run): `rounds.py` gains `cpin_es3_nc1` (365 days; otherwise the `stale_drop` rule unchanged); `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`, `--score --keep-open --status-dir`, cascade2, 3 seeds; `cpin_es3_nc2` for H = austin, sf, seattle and Y = 2017 to 2022 (18 fits), `cpin_es3_nc1` for H = austin only (6 fits); fresh pull 2026-11-19; one process per held city (and one for Austin nc1) at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_floor_i2c.json`, scored by `src/tfol_boot.py --floor` (`floor_summary`, written and unit-tested in this commit before any fit, `tests/test_tfol_floor.py`). nc3 to nc5 come from session 103's 12 SF/Seattle cells (`results/rounds_score_grid_i2c_grid.json`) and session 106's 6 Austin cells (`results/rounds_score_austin_i2c_austin.json`). G4 = {nc2, nc3, nc4, nc5}; oracle = best G4 member on the test year; chooser4 = lowest k6_loss over G4, chooser3 = the same over G3 = {nc3, nc4, nc5}, both scored against the G4 oracle.
Already seen (disclosed): session 92's sweep (`results/rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`) has nc2 for the six 2021 and 2022 cells: worse than nc3 by 2.65, 1.14 (SF), 4.03, 0.95 (Seattle) days, better by 1.96, 2.78 (Austin). Its k6 scores were never computed. The other 12 nc2 cells and every nc1 number are unseen.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days; regret = a rule minus the G4 oracle in a cell):
- (B0, check) The saved rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001 for all 24 fits, and nc2 reproduces session 92's sweep within 0.1 days in the six 2021 and 2022 cells. Prior 85%.
- (B1) nc2 is below nc3 in at least 5 of the 6 Austin cells. Prior 65%.
- (B2) nc2 is above nc3 in at least 10 of the 12 SF/Seattle cells. Prior 70%.
- (B3) chooser4 picks nc2 in at least 10 of the 12 SF/Seattle cells (the Y-6 score's lean to short cuts carries down one step). Prior 60%.
- (B4, primary) Pooled over the 18 cells, chooser4's mean regret exceeds chooser3's: putting the 2-year cut on the chooser's menu makes it worse. Prior 60%.
- (B5) nc1 is above nc2 in at least 4 of the 6 Austin cells (Austin's floor is 2 years, not 1). Prior 55%.
- (B6) Pooled over the 18 cells, nc3 has the lowest mean regret of G4's four fixed cuts. Prior 60%.
Each refuted if it fails. Risks named in advance: the 1-year cut drops every source permit open at D that was issued more than a year earlier, which removes most of Y-2's open tail and may leave the early-stopping set (Y-3) with no open rows at all, so nc1 is a degenerate floor; the nc3 to nc5 cells come from pulls one to three days older (same row counts in sessions 103 to 106); 3 seeds; q0.9 only.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit ab75277e2, before any model run (draft PR #17686). Pull 2026-11-19 14:07 to 14:44 UTC: SF 1,298,572 rows (750,934 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,386,174 (2,187,174): Seattle identical to sessions 103 to 106, SF 60 rows and Austin 496 rows more than session 106's pull (so the nc3 to nc5 cells this is pooled with come from slightly smaller feeds). Fits on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 14:45 to 15:24 UTC, one process per held city and variant at 1 thread; Seattle 2021 and 2022 were moved to their own processes once the Austin fits had freed two cores (same pull, seeds and code, so the same fits), and the Seattle process stopped after 2020. With four processes on four cores a seed took 80 to 350 s, not the 8 to 10 s session 106 reported. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_floor_i2c.json` (24 fits), `results/rounds_score_floor_i2c_floor.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --floor`).
- Test q0.9 per cell (days; nc2, then nc3, nc4, nc5 from sessions 101 to 106): Austin 2017 58.79 (61.77, 77.29, 90.60), 2018 64.41 (69.80, 84.75, 86.57), 2019 64.22 (67.65, 80.06, 84.10), 2020 61.32 (64.87, 78.20, 78.29), 2021 64.82 (66.76, 81.19, 83.93), 2022 62.46 (65.18, 70.98, 81.25); SF 82.50, 89.38, 88.71, 83.57, 73.22, 59.35 for 2017 to 2022; Seattle 80.80, 92.73, 88.73, 107.14, 95.00, 74.97. Austin nc1: 56.66, 61.13, 56.89, 63.25, 62.05, 59.97.
- (B0, check) Refuted: the saved rows reproduce the JSON means for all 24 fits (largest difference 7.6e-6), but nc2 matches session 92's sweep only within 0.22 days in the six 2021 and 2022 cells (SF 2021 73.22 against 73.44; the others within 0.17), not 0.1. The feeds have grown since session 92's pull (2026-11-15); the bar was set too tight for a pull that changes rows.
- (B1) Held: nc2 is below nc3 in all 6 Austin cells, by 1.94 to 5.39 days (mean 62.67 against 66.00).
- (B2) Held: nc2 is above nc3 in all 12 SF/Seattle cells, by 0.46 to 7.39 days (mean regret 5.05 against nc3's 1.73).
- (B3) Refuted: chooser4 picks nc2 in 4 of 12 SF/Seattle cells (SF 2017 to 2020), nc3 in 5, nc4 in 1, nc5 in 2. The Y-6 score's lean to short cuts does carry down one step in SF 2017 to 2020 (k6 ranks the four cuts by length there), but in SF 2021 and 2022 and in Seattle the 2-year cut's Y-6 score is not the lowest.
- (B4, primary) Refuted: pooled over 18 cells chooser4 leaves 1.38 days of regret against the G4 oracle, chooser3 1.72: the 2-year cut on the menu helps. It gains 3.33 days in every Austin cell (picks nc2 in all 6, the oracle each time) and loses 1.15 per SF/Seattle cell (2.06 against 0.91), all of it in SF 2017 to 2020 (0.70, 3.92, 6.96 and 5.60 days).
- (B5) Refuted: nc1 is above nc2 in only 1 of 6 Austin cells (2020, by 1.93); mean 59.99 against 62.67. Per seed nc1 minus nc2 is negative in 13 of 18 seed-cells (from -8.27 to +4.67). The risk named in advance happened: at 1 year every source permit open at D is older than the cut (training is years up to Y-4 and Y-2, early stopping Y-3), so nc1 trains with no open rows (`n_train_open` 0 and `n_es_open` 0 in every fit) and is simply the sources' completed builds.
- (B6) Held: pooled over 18 cells nc3 has the lowest mean regret of G4's fixed cuts (2.27; nc2 3.37, nc4 5.88, nc5 8.26).
- Post hoc (not pre-registered): worst cell over the 18: chooser4 6.96 (SF 2019), chooser3 5.39, nc3 5.46, nc2 11.83, nc4 20.34. The Y-6 score ranks nc1 below nc2 in all 6 Austin years (by 0.10 in 2020, 3.0 to 8.4 otherwise), so a chooser with nc1 on the menu would take it in Austin and be right 5 times of 6 (regret 1.93 in 2020); nc1 was not fitted for SF and Seattle, so that chooser cannot be scored on 18 cells yet.
- What this says: for a target whose builds are shorter than every source's, the borrowed model does best with none of the sources' open permits at all, and every year of staleness allowance added costs Austin about 2 to 4 days (1 year 59.99, 2 years 62.67, 3 years 66.00) before the 4-year step jumps by 12.7. For long-build targets the 2-year cut is never right. The chooser on the target's six-year-old permits finds both facts on its own in Austin; its cost is SF 2017 to 2020, where it still leans short. Advice moves one step: score the 2-, 3-, 4- and 5-year cuts (and, for a city whose builds are short, completed source builds only) on the target's six-year-old permits, and take the lowest; it beats every fixed cut pooled, though its worst city-year (6.96 days) is worse than the fixed 3-year cut's (5.46).
- Limits: one short-build target; 3 seeds and seed spread in Austin of several days (nc1 against nc2 per seed from -8.27 to +4.67), so the 1-versus-2-year order is less settled than the 2-versus-3; q0.9 only; the pooled cells mix pulls one to four days apart; the G4 oracle is over four cuts, so regrets here are not comparable to session 106's 3-cut figures.
Next: (1) nc1 on the 12 SF/Seattle cells, so a chooser over {1, 2, 3, 4, 5} can be scored pooled (pre-register: does it pick nc1 outside Austin?); (2) a guard on the chooser's short lean in SF 2017 to 2020 (e.g. pick the shorter cut only if it wins Y-6 by more than the seed spread), fixed before scoring; (3) a fourth city as target, short-build if one with a completion field and an open licence exists.

## Session 106, 2026-11-19: the 4-year staleness cut does not carry to a short-build target: with Austin as target (sources SF and Seattle) the 3-year cut wins all 6 city-years 2017 to 2022 and the 4-year cut gives up 12.74 days of q0.9 (5-year 18.12, batch-dropped ncrd 21.62); pooled over 18 city-years the 3-year cut leaves 1.16 days of regret against 4.77 for the 4-year cut, and the Y-6 chooser over {3, 4, 5} leaves 0.61 (worst cell 4.44, against 5.46 and 15.53); A0 to A5 all held

Why: sessions 103 to 105 settled on a fixed 4-year staleness cut as the default for borrowing other cities' permits (0.78 days of pooled q0.9 regret against the {3, 4, 5}-year oracle over 12 SF and Seattle city-years), but every one of those cells has a long-build target. Session 100 found that with Austin as target every 5-year rule loses about 16 days to the 3-year cut in 2021 and 2022, and Austin was never fitted at the 4-year cut or in 2017 to 2020. Session 105's next step (2): Austin as target at nc4 and ncrd. This is the first test of the 4-year default on a target it was not chosen on, and of the Y-6 chooser (which lost to nc4 on the 12 cells) when a short-build target is in the pool.
Design (fixed before any model run): unchanged `rounds.py` (`CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`, `--score --keep-open --status-dir`), cascade2, 3 seeds; H = austin (sources SF and Seattle); Y = 2017 to 2022; variants `cpin_es3_nc3`, `cpin_es3_nc4`, `cpin_es3_nc5`, `cpin_es3_ncrd` (24 fits); fresh pull 2026-11-19; one process per variant at 1 thread, merged into `results/rounds_score_austin_i2c.json`; scored by `src/tfol_boot.py --austin` (`austin_summary`, written and unit-tested in this commit before any fit), pooled with session 103's 12 grid cells (`results/rounds_score_grid_i2c_grid.json`) for 18 cells.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed Austin test rows, days; regret = a rule minus the grid oracle in a cell):
- (A0, check) The saved rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001 for all 24 fits, and ncrd's SF and Seattle source thresholds equal `results/robust_cut_i2c.json` within 1e-6 (same as session 105's pull if the row counts match).
- (A1) nc3 is the grid oracle in at least 5 of the 6 Austin cells.
- (A2) Fixed nc4's mean regret over the 6 Austin cells exceeds 2.31 days, its worst SF/Seattle cell: the 4-year default does not carry to a short-build target.
- (A3) Pooled over the 18 cells, nc3 has the lowest mean regret of the three fixed cuts (the default flips once Austin is a target).
- (A4) ncrd's mean Austin regret is at least nc4's (its source cuts, 4.4 to 6.2 years, are longer).
- (A5) The Y-6 chooser over the grid picks nc3 in at least 5 of 6 Austin cells, and its 18-cell mean regret is below every fixed cut's.
Each refuted if it fails. Risks named in advance: session 100's Austin tail models stopped after 3 to 5 rounds in some fits, so cells may be seed noise; with SF and Seattle as the only sources the target's builds are shorter than every source's, so all four rules may be far from a model trained on Austin (not tested here); 3 seeds; q0.9 only; the 12 SF/Seattle cells come from pulls one to three days older.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 2ca2938fc, before any model run (draft PR #17642). Pull 2026-11-19 11:08 to 11:25 UTC: SF 1,298,512 rows (750,890 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,385,678 (2,186,678), the same counts as sessions 103 to 105; status pull 11:08 to 11:15. Fits on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 11:25 to 11:30 UTC, four processes (one per variant) at 1 thread: each Austin-target fit trains on SF and Seattle only and took 8 to 10 s per seed (data loading was most of the five minutes). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_austin_i2c.json` (24 fits), `results/rounds_score_austin_i2c_austin.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --austin`).
- Test q0.9 per Austin cell (days; nc3, nc4, nc5, ncrd): 2017 61.77, 77.29, 90.60, 92.48; 2018 69.80, 84.75, 86.57, 94.87; 2019 67.65, 80.06, 84.10, 94.46; 2020 64.87, 78.20, 78.29, 84.68; 2021 66.76, 81.19, 83.93, 79.17; 2022 65.18, 70.98, 81.25, 80.07. Session 100's nc3 and nc5 for 2021 and 2022 (`results/rounds_score_rep_i2c.json`: 66.76, 83.93; 65.18, 81.25) reproduce to the second decimal.
- (A0, check) Held: the saved rows reproduce the JSON means for all 24 fits (largest difference 7.6e-6), and ncrd's SF and Seattle source cuts equal `robust_cut_i2c.json` exactly (SF 1,687 to 1,857 days, Seattle 2,025, 2,255, 2,159, 1,753, 1,597, 1,652).
- (A1) Held: nc3 is the grid oracle in all 6 Austin cells. Per seed, nc4 minus nc3 is positive in 17 of 18 seed-cells (6.5 to 21.9 days; the exception is 2022 seed 1, -1.52).
- (A2) Held: nc4's mean Austin regret is 12.74 days (mean test q0.9 78.74 against nc3's 66.00), 5.5 times its worst SF/Seattle cell (2.31). nc5's is 18.12.
- (A3) Held: pooled over 18 cells, mean regret nc3 1.16, nc4 4.77, nc5 7.15.
- (A4) Held: ncrd's mean Austin regret is 21.62 days (mean 87.62), above nc4's; it beats nc4 only in 2021 (79.17 against 81.19).
- (A5) Held: the Y-6 chooser picks nc3 in all 6 Austin cells (Austin's own six-year-old permits score nc3 lowest by 6 to 18 days in every year), regret 0 there; pooled over 18 cells its mean regret is 0.61 days, below nc3's 1.16.
- Post hoc (not pre-registered): worst-cell regret over 18 cells: chooser 4.44, nc3 5.46, nc4 15.53, nc5 28.83. The chooser's pooled edge comes entirely from the Austin cells, where it and the fixed 3-year cut agree; on the 12 SF/Seattle cells it still trails nc4 (0.91 against 0.78, session 103). Session 92's sweep (reported, not tested) had the 2-year cut beating the 3-year cut in Austin 2021 and 2022 by 1.95 and 2.78 days, so the grid's floor may itself be too long for Austin.
- What this says: the 4-year cut was chosen on long-build targets and costs about 13 days when the target's builds are shorter than every source's. A fixed rule chosen on the pool's majority is not robust to the target; the target's own six-year-old permits, which lost to the fixed 4-year cut when only SF and Seattle were in the pool, rank the cuts correctly in every Austin year and now give the lowest mean and worst-case regret of any rule tested over the 18 city-years. Practical advice changes: when borrowing other cities' permits, score the 3-, 4- and 5-year cuts on the target's own permits issued six years before the forecast date and take the lowest; if the target has no six-year history, the 3-year cut is the safer fixed default (worst cell 5.46 days), not the 4-year cut.
- Limits: one short-build target (Austin) and only two sources for it; q0.9 only; 3 seeds; the 12 SF/Seattle cells come from pulls one to three days older (same row counts as session 103's); the chooser's pooled win rests on 6 cells where all reasonable rules agree, so it is a robustness result, not evidence that the chooser picks well within SF and Seattle.
Next: (1) extend the grid down to 2 years (and 1) for Austin and score the chooser over {2, 3, 4, 5}, pre-registered over the 18 cells; (2) a fourth city as target (the build-time harness leaves Chicago out, so a new feed is needed: check its completion field and licence, or another city's, first); (3) the paired row bootstrap of ncrd against nc4 in the six Seattle cells (session 105's next step 3).

## Session 105, 2026-11-19: robust per-source staleness cuts still lose to a fixed 4-year cut: pooled over 12 SF and Seattle city-years the 0.95 quantile of recent completions leaves 2.51 days of q0.9 regret against the {3, 4, 5}-year oracle and the 0.99 quantile with batch close-out days dropped 2.00 (fixed 4-year cut 0.78, session 104's plain 0.99 quantile 6.65); dropping 751 batch rows on 9 days brings Seattle's 2018 to 2020 cut from 10.2 years to 6.2, 5.9 and 4.8 years but not under 6 in 2018; post hoc, the short rule suits SF as target (0.95 days) and the batch-dropped rule suits Seattle (0.91 against the 4-year cut's 1.08); M0 held, M1 to M4 refuted

Why: session 104 found a staleness cut set from each source's 0.99 quantile of recent completions loses to the fixed 4-year cut (6.65 against 0.78 days of pooled q0.9 regret), because one batch close-out of old Seattle permits in 2017 moved the tail. Its next step (1): a robust per-source cut, pre-registered before any fit: the 0.95 quantile, or the 0.99 quantile after dropping completion days that close many permits issued over 8 years earlier. Both are fitted here, with their parameters fixed before any threshold was computed on the new pull.
Design (fixed before any threshold or model run): two new variants in `src/rounds.py`, each a cut per source city exactly as session 104's `cpin_es3_ncr` (window: builds completed in the 3 years before D; any source permit open at D for longer than the cut is dropped) except: `cpin_es3_ncr95` takes the 0.95 quantile; `cpin_es3_ncrd` takes the 0.99 quantile after `batch_days` drops every completion on a calendar day on which more than 10 (`BATCH_N`) of that city's completions closed permits issued more than 2,922 days (8 years, `OLD_DAYS`) earlier. Everything else unchanged (`CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`, cascade2, 3 seeds, `--score --keep-open --status-dir`); H = sf, seattle (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin); Y = 2017 to 2022 (12 cells per variant, 24 fits); fresh pull 2026-11-19; one process per held city and variant at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_robust_i2c.json`, scored by `src/tfol_boot.py --robust` (`robust_summary`) against the grid G = {nc3, nc4, nc5} (`results/rounds_score_grid_i2c_grid.json`). `src/robust_cut.py` writes each rule's thresholds per city and year from the pull (`results/robust_cut_i2c.json`), run after this commit.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days; regret = variant minus the G oracle):
- (M0, check) The saved rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001 for both variants, and every threshold a fit reports equals `robust_cut.py`'s.
- (M1) Pooled over the 12 cells, ncr95's mean regret is below fixed nc4's 0.78.
- (M2) Pooled over the 12 cells, ncrd's mean regret is below fixed nc4's 0.78.
- (M3a, M3b) ncr95's and ncrd's worst-cell regret are each at most nc4's 2.31.
- (M4, mechanism) With SF held out, Seattle's source threshold in Y = 2018, 2019, 2020 is under 2,191 days (6 years) under both rules: the 2017 sweep no longer sets the cut.
Each refuted if it fails. Risks named in advance: Seattle's 2017 close-outs may be spread over many days with 10 or fewer old permits each (then `batch_days` misses them and ncrd behaves like ncr, M4 fails for ncrd); the 0.95 quantile may land near 3 years for SF and Austin, so ncr95 may behave like nc3 (session 103: 1.73 days of regret); session 104 found that even outside the sweep cells a per-source cut tied the fixed 5-year cut (1.77 days of regret), so a robust cut may only reach that level; the grid cuts were fitted on pulls one to two days older; 3 seeds; q0.9 only.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 16e0ed0b7, before any threshold or model run (draft PR #17586). Pull 2026-11-19 07:39 to 07:52 UTC: SF 1,298,512 rows (750,890 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,385,678 (2,186,678), the same counts as sessions 103 and 104. `src/robust_cut.py` ran next (thresholds only, no fits); fits on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 07:53 to about 08:45 UTC, four processes (held city by variant) at 1 thread. Outputs: `results/robust_cut_i2c.json`, `results/rounds_score_robust_i2c.json` (24 fits), `results/rounds_score_robust_i2c_robust.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --robust`).
- Thresholds (days, `robust_cut.py`; the fits report the same to the digit). Session 104's ncr recomputed on this pull matches its fits exactly (SF 1,765 to 1,966, Seattle 2,025, 3,721, 3,762, 3,758, 1,597, 1,652, Austin 1,684 falling to 1,133). ncr95: SF 700 to 1,015 (1.9 to 2.8 years), Seattle 753, then 1,105 to 1,146, then 928 and 1,002, Austin 508 to 550 (1.4 to 1.5 years): every cut is shorter than the 3-year cut for Austin and SF. ncrd: SF 1,687 to 1,857, Seattle 2,025, 2,255, 2,159, 1,753, 1,597, 1,652, Austin 1,661 falling to 1,097. `batch_days` drops 751 rows on 9 days from each of Seattle's 2018 to 2020 windows (of 18,727 to 19,342 completions), none in its other windows; it also fires in SF (98 to 578 rows on 1 to 5 days in 2017 to 2021) and Austin (276 to 1,052 rows on 1 to 4 days in five of six years).
- Test q0.9 per cell (days; ncr95, ncrd; nc3, nc4, nc5 from sessions 101 to 103): SF 2017 82.63, 92.17 (81.80, 83.17, 90.66); 2018 86.63, 92.32 (86.19, 85.46, 89.13); 2019 83.88, 84.02 (83.31, 81.75, 82.17); 2020 79.00, 77.91 (79.05, 78.12, 77.97); 2021 70.94, 69.71 (70.79, 71.33, 72.35); 2022 58.55, 58.34 (58.16, 59.04, 61.21); Seattle 2017 81.48, 80.66 (80.34, 79.91, 81.73); 2018 92.50, 88.32 (89.68, 88.48, 88.60); 2019 83.80, 80.04 (82.25, 80.30, 80.79); 2020 100.66, 97.07 (99.75, 97.25, 95.31); 2021 91.27, 88.34 (91.07, 87.82, 85.61); 2022 74.23, 70.55 (73.86, 72.22, 69.91).
- (M0, check) Held: the saved rows reproduce the JSON means for all 24 fits (largest difference 7.6e-6), and every reported threshold equals `robust_cut.py`'s (difference 0).
- (M1) Refuted: ncr95's mean regret is 2.51 days (mean test q0.9 82.13; nc4 80.40). It beats no grid cut in any cell and is within 1.0 day of the oracle in 3 of 12.
- (M2) Refuted: ncrd's mean regret is 2.00 days (mean 81.62), within 1.0 day in 7 of 12; it beats every grid cut in 4 cells (SF 2020 by 0.06, SF 2021 by 1.08, Seattle 2018 by 0.16, Seattle 2019 by 0.27).
- (M3a, M3b) Refuted: worst-cell regret ncr95 5.66 days (Seattle 2021), ncrd 10.37 (SF 2017), against nc4's 2.31.
- (M4) Refuted: under ncr95 Seattle's source cut in 2018 to 2020 is 1,105 to 1,146 days, but under ncrd it is 2,255 in 2018 (over 6 years), 2,159 in 2019 and 1,753 in 2020. The first named risk happened in part: some of the 2017 close-outs fall on days with 10 or fewer old permits, so the batch rule removes the sweep's peak, not all of it.
- Post hoc (not pre-registered), split by target: with SF held out (Seattle and Austin as sources) ncr95's mean regret is 0.95 days (nc4 0.49) and ncrd's 3.09; with Seattle held out (SF and Austin as sources) ncr95's is 4.07 and ncrd's 0.91, below nc4's 1.08 (ncrd beats nc4 in 4 of those 6 cells, by 0.17 days on average). ncrd's SF loss sits in 2017 (10.37) and 2018 (6.87), where its cuts (Seattle 5.5 and 6.2 years, Austin 4.5 and 3.8) are as long as session 104's ncr's and the SF target wants the 3-year cut; it has nothing to do with the sweep in 2017 (the 2017 window ends before it). Excluding SF 2017 and 2018, ncrd's mean regret is 0.68 days against nc4's 0.80 over the same 10 cells. That is selection after the fact; it is not evidence that ncrd should be used.
- What this says: across sessions 93, 104 and 105, four per-source quantile cuts (all-years 0.99, recent 0.99, recent 0.95, recent 0.99 with batch days dropped) have each lost to a fixed 4-year cut pooled over the same cells. The quantile level sets the cut's length (0.95 gives 1.4 to 2.8 years, 0.99 gives 3 to 6), and the best length depends on the target (SF wants short, Seattle long, sessions 92 and 103), which a statistic of the sources cannot see. Robustness to batch close-outs fixes the worst failure (6.65 down to 2.00 days) but not the target dependence. For a practitioner borrowing other cities' permits: the fixed 4-year cut remains the default.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; one model class; BATCH_N = 10 and OLD_DAYS = 8 years fixed in advance and not swept (a sweep chosen on these test years would be selection); grid fits from pulls one to two days older (same row counts as session 103's pull); no row bootstrap of ncrd against nc4 in the Seattle cells (nc4's rows from session 103 were not saved here), so a 0.17-day mean edge is within seed spread.
Next: (1) a cut set from the target, not the sources: the target's own open stock on D (Seattle's share open past four years rose 9 points 2017 to 2022, session 104) predicting which grid cut wins, pre-registered over the 12 cells; (2) Austin as target at nc4 and ncrd (session 100: the 3-year cut won there by about 16 days); (3) a paired row bootstrap of ncrd against nc4 in the six Seattle cells, refitting nc4 with rows saved.

## Session 104, 2026-11-19: a staleness cut set from each source's recent completions does not beat a fixed 4-year cut: pooled over 12 SF and Seattle city-years it leaves 6.65 days of q0.9 regret against the {3, 4, 5}-year oracle (fixed 4-year cut 0.78), because one Seattle close-out sweep in 2017 (467 permits older than 8 years) pushes Seattle's 0.99 quantile from 4.4 to 10.3 years for three years and costs SF 19 to 22 days in 2018 to 2020; outside those cells it behaves like the 5-year cut; N0 held, N1 to N4 refuted

Why: session 103 found no year-invariant best staleness cut (the 4-year cut is the hedge, 0.78 days of pooled regret against the {3, 4, 5}-year oracle) and nothing the target holds on D picks the right one. Its next step (1): set the cut from the sources' own recent behaviour. Session 93's `cpin_es3_ncq` took each source city's 0.99 quantile of build days over all its years completed by D (Austin and SF about 3.2 years, Seattle 6.4), fitted only in 2021 and 2022. Here the quantile is taken only over builds *completed* in the 3 years before D, so the cut tracks how long recently finished permits took.
Design (fixed before any model run): new variant `cpin_es3_ncr` in `src/rounds.py` (`recent_threshold`: per source city, the 0.99 quantile of issue-to-completion days over its permits issued before D whose completion date falls in [D minus 3 years, D); any source permit open at D for longer is dropped, as in `stale_drop`); everything else unchanged (`CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`, cascade2, 3 seeds, `--score --keep-open --status-dir`); H = sf, seattle (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin); Y = 2017 to 2022 (12 cells); fresh pull 2026-11-19; one process per cell at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_recent_i2c.json`, scored by `src/tfol_boot.py --recent` against the grid G = {nc3, nc4, nc5} from sessions 101 to 103 (`results/rounds_score_grid_i2c_grid.json`). Regret = ncr's test q0.9 minus the G oracle's (negative if ncr beats every grid cut).
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (N0, check) The saved ncr rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001, and the thresholds the fits report equal `recent_threshold` recomputed from the pull.
- (N1) Pooled over the 12 cells, ncr's mean regret is below fixed nc4's 0.78 days.
- (N2) ncr is within 1.0 day of the G oracle in at least 10 of the 12 cells.
- (N3) ncr's worst-cell regret is at most nc4's 2.31 days.
- (N4, mechanism) With Seattle held out, the mean source threshold over Y = 2020 to 2022 exceeds that over 2017 to 2019 (the cut lengthens in the years Seattle wanted the 5-year cut).
Refuted if any part fails. Risks named in advance: recently completed builds include late administrative close-outs of old permits, so the 0.99 quantile may sit far above 5 years (then ncr behaves like nc8, which session 92 found 20 days worse); the grid cuts were fitted on pulls one to two days older (a few dozen rows differ), so regrets under about 0.3 days are within that drift; 3 seeds; q0.9 only.
Descriptive (not a hypothesis, session 103's next step 2): the share of each city's permits issued before D that are still open at D after more than four years, per Y.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 3a0237296, before any model run (draft PR #17501). Pull 2026-11-19 02:07 to 02:32 UTC: SF 1,298,512 rows (750,890 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,385,678 (2,186,678): the same counts as session 103's pull, so the grid fits compare like for like. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 02:33 to about 03:15 UTC, four processes (SF and Seattle, 2017 to 2019 and 2020 to 2022) at 1 thread (`CPIN_THREADS=1`). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_recent_i2c.json` (12 fits), `results/rounds_score_recent_i2c_recent.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --recent`), `results/open_share_i2c.json` (`src/open_share.py`, computed before the fits).
- Source thresholds (days; the fits report the same values as `open_share.py` to the digit): SF as source 1,765 to 1,966 in every year (4.8 to 5.4 years); Austin 1,684 (2017), 1,401, 1,208, 1,155, 1,120, 1,133 (falling to 3.1 years); Seattle 2,025 (2017), then 3,721, 3,762, 3,758 (10.2 to 10.3 years) in 2018 to 2020, then 1,597 and 1,652 (4.4 to 4.5 years) in 2021 and 2022. Session 93's all-years cut, for comparison: SF 1,081 to 1,240, Austin 1,138 to 1,145, Seattle 1,640 to 2,731.
- ncr test q0.9 per cell (days; nc3, nc4, nc5 beside it): SF 2017 91.99 (81.80, 83.17, 90.66), 2018 107.61 (86.19, 85.46, 89.13), 2019 101.09 (83.31, 81.75, 82.17), 2020 100.27 (79.05, 78.12, 77.97), 2021 69.75 (70.79, 71.33, 72.35), 2022 58.50 (58.16, 59.04, 61.21); Seattle 2017 82.17 (80.34, 79.91, 81.73), 2018 88.64 (89.68, 88.48, 88.60), 2019 80.66 (82.25, 80.30, 80.79), 2020 96.37 (99.75, 97.25, 95.31), 2021 87.40 (91.07, 87.82, 85.61), 2022 70.75 (73.86, 72.22, 69.91).
- (N0, check) Held: the saved rows reproduce the JSON means (largest difference 7.6e-6) and every reported threshold equals `recent_threshold` recomputed from the pull (difference 0).
- (N1) Refuted: mean regret 6.65 days against nc4's 0.78 (mean test q0.9 86.27 against nc4 80.40, nc5 81.29, nc3 81.36).
- (N2) Refuted: within 1.0 day of the grid oracle in 5 of 12 cells. It beats every grid cut in one (SF 2021, by 1.04 days).
- (N3) Refuted: worst-cell regret 22.30 days (SF 2020) against nc4's 2.31.
- (N4) Refuted: with Seattle held out, the mean source threshold falls from 1,653 days (2017 to 2019) to 1,485 (2020 to 2022), driven by Austin's shortening cut; the rule moves the opposite way to what Seattle's test years wanted.
- Mechanism (post hoc, `src/open_share.py` extended after the fits, same pull): Seattle's feed records 467 completions in calendar 2017 of permits issued more than 8 years earlier (7 to 40 in every other year 2013 to 2022). That one batch is 2.7% of Seattle's completions in each of the 3-year windows ending 1 January 2018, 2019 and 2020 (0.3% in the windows ending 2017, 2021 and 2022), enough to move the 0.99 quantile past 10 years. SF and Austin show no such batch (0.5 to 0.7% and 0.3 to 0.4% of each window over 8 years, every year). With Seattle as a source kept open for 10 years, SF's models early-stop after 13 to 15 rounds in 2019 and lose 19 to 22 days to the grid in 2018 to 2020.
- Post hoc, outside the three sweep cells (9 cells): ncr's mean regret is 1.77 days, the same as the fixed 5-year cut's (1.77) and above the 4-year cut's (1.03); over the 6 Seattle-held cells it ties the 4-year cut (1.08 each; 5-year cut 0.41). So even without the sweep a per-source quantile cut buys nothing over a fixed rule.
- Descriptive (session 103's next step 2, `results/open_share_i2c.json`): of Seattle permits issued before D and still open on D, the share open more than four years grows from 0.458 (2017) to 0.475 (2019) and 0.549 (2022), and the open stock from 19,423 to 27,340. SF's open stock is far staler (0.83 to 0.87 over four years, 403,390 to 465,573 permits, mostly never closed); Austin's is flat (0.63 to 0.66). Seattle's own backlog is ageing, which fits its test years wanting a longer cut, but the sources' cut is what the model sees, and nothing in the sources tracks it.
- What this says (post hoc): a cut learned from the sources' own completion records inherits their bookkeeping. A tail quantile is set by the rarest completions, and in open permit feeds those are administrative close-outs of old permits, which arrive in batches. A fixed 4-year cut stays the default. Any data-driven cut needs a robust statistic (a lower quantile, or completions deduplicated by close-out day) and a check against batch close-outs before it is trusted.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; one model class; one quantile (0.99) and one window (3 years), both fixed in advance, so a lower quantile or longer window may behave better (not tested here: picking them after seeing these results would be selection on the test years); the grid's nc3 and nc5 fits for 2019 to 2022 came from session 101's pull (a few dozen rows fewer).
Next: (1) a robust per-source cut, pre-registered before any fit: the 0.95 quantile, or the 0.99 quantile after dropping completion days that close more than N permits issued over 8 years earlier; (2) Austin as target at nc4 (session 100: the 3-year cut won there by about 16 days); (3) a model of the target's own ageing open stock (Seattle's share open past four years rose 9 points from 2017 to 2022) as a covariate rather than a cut.

## Session 103, 2026-11-18: a 4-year staleness cut is the robust default: pooled over all 12 SF and Seattle city-years 2017 to 2022 it leaves 0.78 days of regret against the {3, 4, 5}-year oracle (5-year cut 1.67, 3-year cut 1.73, the six-year-old-permit chooser 0.91), is never the worst of the three in any cell, and its worst cell costs 2.31 days against 8.86 and 5.46 for the 5- and 3-year cuts; in 2019 to 2022 alone the 5-year cut still edges it (78.16 against 78.48 days); G0, G2 held, G1, G3, G4 refuted

Why: session 102 found the 4-year cut best of the staleness grid in 2017 and 2018 (84.25 days against 87.53 for the 5-year cut) but fit it only there, and named as next steps (1) nc4 in the 8 cells of 2019 to 2022 and (2) a cut chosen per deployment from the target's Y-6 score over a grid (nc3, nc4, nc5), scored pooled over all 12 cells. Both come from one set of fits: nc4 with the target cohorts scored.
Design (fixed before any model run): unchanged `rounds.py` (`CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`); variant `cpin_es3_nc4` only; H = sf, seattle (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin); Y = 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull 2026-11-18; `--score --keep-open --status-dir`; one process per cell at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_grid_i2c.json`. nc3 and nc5 for these cells are session 101's fits (`results/rounds_score_oos_i2c.json`); 2017 and 2018 come from session 102 (`results/rounds_score_early_i2c.json`). `src/tfol_boot.py --grid` (`grid_summary`) scores the grid G = {nc3, nc4, nc5}: oracle = the member of G with the lowest test q0.9 in a cell; the grid chooser picks the member of G with the lowest k6_loss (q0.9 pinball on the target's own Y-6 permits completed by D = 1 January Y).
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (G0, check) The saved nc4 rows reproduce the JSON means within 0.001, and the nc4 fits' target-cohort sizes equal session 101's for the same cells.
- (G1) Over the 8 cells of 2019 to 2022, nc4's mean test q0.9 is below both nc3's and nc5's.
- (G2) Pooled over all 12 SF and Seattle city-years 2017 to 2022, fixed nc4 has the lowest mean regret (against the G oracle) of the three fixed cuts.
- (G3) Fixed nc4 is within 1.0 day of the G oracle in at least 10 of the 12 cells.
- (G4) Pooled over the 12 cells, the grid chooser's mean regret is below fixed nc4's.
Refuted if any part fails. Risks named in advance: k6_loss favoured the shorter tail in 10 of 12 cells among the session-98 candidates, so the chooser may simply pick nc3 (then G4 likely fails); nc4 may interpolate between nc3 and nc5 cell by cell and win no year pair outright (G1 needs it to beat both on average, not in each cell); 2020 is the first COVID year; the 2017 and 2018 nc4 fits came from a pull a few hours earlier than this one.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit dd1f82410, before any model run (draft PR #17396). Pull 2026-11-18 19:09 to 19:39 UTC: SF 1,298,512 rows (750,890 completed), Seattle 194,353 (108,302), Austin 2,186,678 completed; a few dozen rows more than session 102's pull. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 19:40 to 20:05 UTC, four processes (one per held city and year pair) at 1 thread. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_grid_i2c.json` (8 fits), `results/rounds_score_grid_i2c_grid.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --grid`).
- nc4 test q0.9 per cell (days; nc3 and nc5 from session 101 beside it): SF 2019 81.75 (83.31, 82.17), SF 2020 78.12 (79.05, 77.97), SF 2021 71.33 (70.79, 72.35), SF 2022 59.04 (58.16, 61.21); Seattle 2019 80.30 (82.25, 80.79), 2020 97.25 (99.75, 95.31), 2021 87.82 (91.07, 85.61), 2022 72.22 (73.86, 69.91).
- (G0, check) Held: the saved nc4 rows reproduce the JSON means (largest difference 7.6e-6), and the Y-6 cohort sizes equal session 101's in all 8 cells (SF 33,348 to 37,230 rows; Seattle 6,681 to 8,042).
- (G1) Refuted: over 2019 to 2022, nc4's mean is 78.48 days, below nc3's 79.78 but above nc5's 78.16. nc4 is the grid oracle in SF 2019 and Seattle 2019 only; Seattle 2020 to 2022 and SF 2020 want the 5-year cut, SF 2021 and 2022 the 3-year cut.
- (G2) Held: pooled over 12 cells, mean regret against the grid oracle is nc4 0.78, nc5 1.67, nc3 1.73. nc4 is the oracle in 5 of 12 cells (nc5 in 4, nc3 in 3).
- (G3) Refuted: nc4 is within 1.0 day of the oracle in 8 of 12 cells (needed 10); the misses are SF 2017 (1.37), Seattle 2020 (1.94), 2021 (2.21) and 2022 (2.31).
- (G4) Refuted: the k6 chooser over the grid leaves 0.91 days of regret (6 hits of 12), above fixed nc4's 0.78. It picks nc3 in 9 cells, nc5 in 2 (Seattle 2021, 2022), nc4 in 1 (Seattle 2017). The Y-6 score still ranks the cuts by length (nc3 below nc4 below nc5) in all six SF cells and in Seattle 2018 to 2020.
- Post hoc (not pre-registered): nc4 is never the worst of the three cuts in any of the 12 cells (nc3 is the worst in 7, nc5 in 5). Worst-cell regret: nc4 2.31, chooser 4.44, nc3 5.46, nc5 8.86. Per seed, nc4 minus nc5 is negative in all 3 seeds in SF 2017, 2018, 2019, 2021, 2022 and Seattle 2017, positive in all 3 in Seattle 2020 to 2022.
- What this says (post hoc): there is no year-invariant best cut, and nothing the target holds on D picks the right one (sessions 100 to 103). Among fixed rules, the middle cut is the hedge: it gives up 0.32 days per cell against the 5-year cut in 2019 to 2022 and saves 7.49 and 3.67 days against it in SF 2017 and 2018, while staying within 2.31 days of the best cut everywhere. For a practitioner borrowing other cities' permits, drop source permits open more than four years. Seattle 2020 to 2022 drift toward longer cuts (a trend worth a model, not a fixed rule).
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; one model class (cascade2); the 2017 and 2018 cells' nc4 fits came from session 102's pull; the oracle is over the grid of three cuts, so regrets are relative to it, not to the best possible cut; no row bootstrap for nc4 against the other cuts (session 101's nc3 and nc5 rows were not kept), so cell-level differences under about 1 day are within seed spread.
Next: (1) a cut that moves with the sources' own recent completions (e.g. the 0.95 or 0.99 quantile of build days among source permits completed in the last three years before D; session 93's ncq used all years); (2) the Seattle 2020 to 2022 drift: does the share of Seattle permits open past four years grow after 2019?; (3) Austin as target at nc4 (session 100: the 3-year cut won there by about 16 days over the 5-year cut).

## Session 102, 2026-11-18: test years 2017 and 2018 side with 2021 and 2022, not 2019 and 2020: the 3-year cut wins in SF by 8.86 and 2.94 days and in Seattle 2017, the six-year-old-permit selector picks it in all 4 cells (regret 0.29 days, against 3.32 for the fixed 5-year cut), a 4-year cut is the best fixed rule (84.25) and a 6-year cut costs 7.5 days over the 5-year; pooled over all 12 SF and Seattle city-years the selector leaves 0.81 days of regret against 1.53 for the best fixed rule; E0, E3 held, E1, E2, E4, E5 refuted

Why: session 100's next step (3) asked whether 2021 and 2022 or 2019 and 2020 are the unusual pair of test years, and session 101 added a finer staleness grid (4 and 6 years) to see whether the 5-year cut sits on a plateau. Test years 2017 and 2018 come before every year any of the four source rules or the target-cohort selector was looked at.
- Method: unchanged `rounds.py` (`CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, `CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, `CPIN_ROWS_DIR`), with two new variants `cpin_es3_nc4` and `cpin_es3_nc6` (the staleness rule at 1,461 and 2,191 days). `src/tfol_boot.py RESULTS ROWS_DIR --early` (`early_summary`) picks by k6_loss among the four session-98 candidates only, scores the pick on the test year, resamples rows (B = 2,000, iid, seed 100) and reports nc4 and nc6 beside them, outside the pick and the oracle.
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; variants nc3, nc4, nc5, nc6, nc5_stamp, nc5_stamp_len; H = sf, seattle (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin); Y = 2017, 2018; 3 seeds; q0.9 only; fresh pull 2026-11-18; `--score --keep-open --status-dir`; one process per cell at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_early_i2c.json` and `_boot.json`. Oracle = the lowest test q0.9 among the four candidates.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (E0, check) The saved rows reproduce every JSON mean within 0.001.
- (E1) In both Seattle cells the oracle is a 5-year rule (nc5, stamp or stamp_len), as in all four Seattle years so far.
- (E2) In both SF cells nc5 minus nc3 on the test year is below zero with its 95% row-bootstrap interval below zero: 2021 and 2022 are the unusual pair.
- (E3) k6_loss picks nc3 in at least 3 of the 4 cells (the selector's bias toward the shorter tail persists).
- (E4) The fixed nc5 rule's mean regret over the 4 cells is at most 0.5 days and below nc3's.
- (E5, plateau) nc4's and nc6's mean test q0.9 over the 4 cells are each within 0.5 days of nc5's.
Refuted if any part fails. Risks named in advance: the Y-6 cohorts are 2011 and 2012 and the sources' training years reach back into the 2008 to 2012 slump, so cohort drift may be larger than in later cells; Seattle's older cohorts may be thin; the 6-year cut keeps permits issued before 2011 (Y = 2017) that the feeds may hold few of, so nc6 may equal nc5 where nothing is open between 5 and 6 years.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 38fdba6f1, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-18 12:40 to 13:06 UTC: SF 1,298,445 rows (750,844 completed), Seattle 194,330 (108,281), Austin 2,385,678 (2,186,678); each a few dozen to a few hundred rows more than sessions 95 to 101's pull. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 13:07 to about 15:35 UTC, one process per cell at 1 thread (about 2.5 times slower per fit than session 101 reported). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_early_i2c.json` (24 fits), `results/rounds_score_early_i2c_boot.json` (`tfol_boot.py ... --early`).
- Test q0.9 per cell (days; nc3, nc4, nc5, nc6, stamp, stamp_len): SF 2017 81.80, 83.17, 90.66, 102.90, 90.97, 89.79; SF 2018 86.19, 85.46, 89.13, 97.67, 90.27, 88.98; Seattle 2017 80.34, 79.91, 81.73, 86.94, 81.26, 81.29; Seattle 2018 89.68, 88.48, 88.60, 92.58, 88.53, 89.57. Completed test rows: 22,523, 23,677, 6,134, 5,715; completed Y-6 target rows 19,044, 19,349, 4,589, 4,868.
- (E0, check) Held: the saved rows reproduce every JSON mean (largest difference below 1e-3).
- (E1) Refuted: Seattle 2017's oracle is nc3 (nc5 minus nc3 +1.39, 95% row interval +0.34 to +2.43); Seattle 2018's is stamp (nc5 minus nc3 -1.08, -2.23 to -0.00).
- (E2) Refuted, strongly: in SF the 3-year cut wins both years, nc5 minus nc3 +8.86 (+8.02, +9.62) and +2.94 (+2.15, +3.73). 2019 and 2020 are the odd pair, not 2021 and 2022.
- (E3) Held: k6_loss picks nc3 in all 4 cells (12 of 12 seeds; at least 99.6% of row resamples on the same side), the oracle in 3.
- (E4) Refuted: mean regret over the 4 cells, nc3 0.29, stamp_len 3.19, nc5 3.32, stamp 3.54; the k6 selector 0.29.
- (E5) Refuted: mean test q0.9 nc4 84.25, nc3 84.50, nc5 87.53, nc6 95.02. The 5-year cut is not on a plateau: the 4-year cut is 3.28 days better and the 6-year cut 7.49 days worse. nc4 is the lowest of the four staleness cuts in 3 of 4 cells (nc3 in SF 2017).
- Pooled, post hoc (`pooled_post_hoc`: these 4 cells plus session 101's 8, the same four candidates): over 12 SF and Seattle city-years 2017 to 2022 the k6 selector leaves 0.81 days of mean regret and is the oracle in 7; the fixed rules leave nc5 1.53, nc3 1.60, stamp_len 1.89, stamp 1.97. The 3-year cut is the oracle in 5 of 12 (SF 2017, 2018, 2021, 2022; Seattle 2017); the selector picks the 3-year cut in 10 of 12, and its misses are all four 2019 and 2020 cells plus Seattle 2018.
- What this says (post hoc): sessions 100 and 101 read 2019 and 2020 as the out-of-sample truth and the selector as broken. With 2017 and 2018 added, those two years are the exception: the shorter cut wins in SF in four of six years, and the selector's bias toward it costs little outside 2019 and 2020. The selector is the best of the five options pooled, but by 0.7 days, with misses concentrated in two adjacent years, so it is a default, not a solved problem. Separately, the staleness cut has a sharp optimum near 3 to 4 years in these early years; it is a parameter worth choosing per year, not fixing at 5.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; iid row bootstrap (session 101's caveat: a week-block bootstrap would widen the intervals); nc4 and nc6 were fit only in these 4 cells, so the grid result does not cover 2019 to 2022; the pooled view is post hoc and pools cells whose candidate rules were found on 2021 and 2022; for Y = 2017 the sources' training years reach into the 2008 to 2012 slump.
Next: (1) fit nc4 in the 8 cells of 2019 to 2022 (is 4 years the better default everywhere, or only before 2019?); (2) a staleness cut chosen per deployment from the target's Y-6 score over a grid (nc3, nc4, nc5), scored pooled over all 12 cells; (3) what made 2019 and 2020 different (the 2013 and 2014 Y-6 cohorts scored by the selector, or the test years' own mix); session 100's (1) on Austin stands.

## Session 101, 2026-11-17: the row bootstrap session 100 asked for: the target-cohort selector is stable and wrong, not noisy; in all 8 SF and Seattle city-years at least 96% of 2,000 permit resamples keep the Y-6 pick on the same side, and the test-year preference for the 5-year cut in 2019 and 2020 has every 95% interval below zero; an independent rerun reproduces session 100's 2019 and 2020 fits to the digit; R0, R5 held, R1 to R4 refuted (as session 100 found)

Why: this run was pre-registered in parallel with session 100 (commit 74d901bd4, 04:28 UTC, before any model run and before session 100's PR #16810 merged; neither run saw the other). It tested the same new cells (SF and Seattle, Y = 2019, 2020) and added what session 100 lacked and named as its next step (2): per-row predictions, so a paired bootstrap can say whether the selector's picks are noisy (right on average, flipping on resampled permits) or biased (stable and wrong). It also refit session 99's 2021 and 2022 cells to save their rows.
- Method: `rounds.py` with `CPIN_ROWS_DIR=DIR` writes, per cell and variant, the q0.9 predictions per seed on the completed test rows and on each target cohort (`DIR/<held>_<Y>_<variant>.npz`, outside git). `src/tfol_boot.py RESULTS ROWS_DIR` picks by k6_loss per cell, scores the pick on the test year, checks the rows against the JSON's means, and resamples rows (B = 2,000, iid over permits, seed 100, fixed before any run) to report how often the pick and its side (3-year cut against a 5-year rule) change, on the Y-6 score and on the test year.
Design (fixed before any model run): session 99's design (cascade2 class; candidates nc3, nc5, nc5_stamp, nc5_stamp_len; q0.9 only; 3 seeds; `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, Y-3, Y-5, Y-6 cohorts); H = sf, seattle; Y = 2019, 2020, 2021, 2022; fresh pull 2026-11-17; four processes at 1 thread. Outputs `results/rounds_score_oos_i2c.json` (32 fits) and `results/rounds_score_oos_i2c_boot.json`.
Predictions, as committed (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (R0, check) The 2021 and 2022 refits' test q0.9 and k6_loss equal session 99's within 0.05 days, and the saved rows reproduce every JSON mean within 0.001.
- (R1) On Y = 2019 and 2020 the oracle is nc3 in both SF cells and a 5-year rule in both Seattle cells.
- (R2) k6_loss picks nc3 in both new SF cells and a 5-year rule in both new Seattle cells.
- (R3) k6_loss's mean test q0.9 over the 4 new cells is lower than every fixed candidate's.
- (R4) k6_loss's mean regret over the 4 new cells is at most 1.0 day.
- (R5) In all 8 cells, at least 80% of row resamples keep the k6 pick on the same side.
(R1 to R4 are session 100's H1 to H4 in other words; they are reported for completeness, and session 100 owns them.)

Outcome. Pull 2026-11-17 04:08 to 04:28 UTC (Chicago, not used, grew by 8 rows); row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) and dated completions (750,797, 108,259, 2,186,393) identical to sessions 95 to 100. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 04:29 to 05:29 UTC.
- (R0, check) Held: the 16 refits of 2021 and 2022 equal session 99's test q0.9 and k6_loss to every printed digit (largest difference 0.000); the saved rows reproduce every JSON mean within 8e-6. The 16 fits of 2019 and 2020 equal session 100's (`results/rounds_score_rep_i2c.json`) to every printed digit as well, from a separate session, pull and process layout.
- (R1 to R4) Refuted, with session 100's numbers: the oracle is a 5-year rule in all 4 new cells, k6_loss picks nc3 in all 4 (12 of 12 seeds), mean regret 2.15 days, mean test q0.9 86.09 (worst fixed rule).
- Test-year nc5 minus nc3 with a 95% row-bootstrap interval (days): SF 2019 -1.14 (-1.75, -0.55), SF 2020 -1.09 (-1.76, -0.44), SF 2021 +1.56 (+0.94, +2.18), SF 2022 +3.04 (+2.48, +3.58); Seattle 2019 -1.46 (-2.80, -0.15), 2020 -4.44 (-6.18, -2.77), 2021 -5.46 (-6.70, -4.27), 2022 -3.95 (-5.15, -2.85). Every interval excludes zero: SF's switch between the year pairs is not resampling noise.
- (R5) Held: the share of resamples keeping the k6 pick's side is 100% in all four SF cells and 100%, 98.7%, 96.2%, 99.1% in Seattle 2019 to 2022. The 95% interval of the k6 nc5 minus nc3 score excludes zero in 7 of 8 cells (Seattle 2021: -1.71 to +0.08). The score's sign matches the test year's in 4 of 8 cells, exactly session 99's four.
- Over the 8 SF and Seattle cells: fixed nc5 78.16, stamp 78.71, stamp_len 78.77, nc3 79.78, k6 selector 78.61, per-cell oracle 77.53.
- What this adds (post hoc): session 100 showed the selector misses on new years; the bootstrap shows why it is not worth tuning. Its score is precisely estimated on 5,600 to 22,500 completed rows per cell and stably favours the shorter tail in 6 of 8 cells, while the test years' preference, also precisely estimated, flips with the year pair in SF. More rows or more seeds would not rescue it: the six-year-old cohort answers a different question (how 2013 to 2016 builds ended) from the one the test year asks. With session 100's Austin cells, the fixed 5-year cut is the default for SF- and Seattle-like targets and the 3-year cut for Austin-like ones; nothing found so far tells which a new target is.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; the bootstrap resamples permits iid and ignores within-week and within-project correlation, so a block bootstrap would give wider intervals (the 7-of-8 exclusions and the 96% stability floor are upper bounds on confidence); Austin cells not rerun with rows; 2020 is the first COVID year.
Next: session 100's (1) and (3) stand (why Austin wants the short cut; test years 2017 and 2018). Added: a week-block version of `tfol_boot.py` (the rows carry issue dates in the frame order, so the week key is one join away), and a finer fixed grid (4 and 6 years) across all 10 cells to see whether 5 years is a plateau for SF and Seattle.

## Session 100, 2026-11-17: session 99's Y-6 target selector does not replicate: on test years 2019 and 2020 a 5-year rule beats the 3-year cut in San Francisco and Seattle alike (all 4 cells), yet every target-cohort score (Y-3, Y-5, Y-6, Y-8, plain or IPCW) still picks the 3-year cut (mean regret 2.15 days against the fixed 5-year cut's 0.12); with Austin as the target the 3-year cut wins by 16.6 days and every selector finds it; pooled over 10 cells k6_loss is the oracle in 6 and leaves 0.86 days of regret, against 1.80 for always taking the 3-year cut; H5 held, H1, H2, H3, H4, H6 refuted

Why: session 99 found that scoring the four candidate source rules on the held city's own permits issued six years before D and completed by D picks the per-cell oracle in all 4 city-years (SF and Seattle, 2021 and 2022), but called 4 of 4 suggestive, not a test, and named "more cells: Austin held out, and test years 2019 and 2020" as its second next step. This run adds six cells the selector has never seen and no earlier session tuned the candidate rules on.
- Method: unchanged `rounds.py` with `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`, plus `CPIN_TGT_KS=3,5,6,8` (the Y-8 cohort as a secondary selector, session 99's third next step in its simplest form). `rounds.py x --summarise-rep FILE` (`replicate_summary`) scores every selector on the new cells, on Austin, and pooled with session 99's four cells (`results/rounds_score_tfol_i2c.json`).
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; candidates nc3, nc5, nc5_stamp, nc5_stamp_len (session 98's `TSEL`); new cells SF and Seattle at Y = 2019 and 2020 (sources: the other two of SF, Seattle, Austin) and Austin held out at Y = 2021 and 2022 (sources SF and Seattle); 3 seeds; q0.9 only; fresh pull 2026-11-17; one process per cell at 1 thread; output `results/rounds_score_rep_i2c.json`. Primary selector k6_loss, as in session 99.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days; oracle = the candidate with the lowest test q0.9 in that cell):
- (H1, the city split) In the new cells the oracle is nc3 in both SF years and a 5-year rule (nc5, stamp or stamp_len) in both Seattle years.
- (H2) k6_loss picks the oracle in at least 3 of the 4 new SF/Seattle cells.
- (H3) k6_loss's mean regret over the 4 new SF/Seattle cells is at most 1.0 day.
- (H4) k6_loss's mean test q0.9 over the 4 new SF/Seattle cells is at most every fixed candidate's.
- (H5) Austin held out: k6_loss's mean regret over its 2 cells is at most 1.0 day.
- (H6, pooled) Over all 10 cells (4 new, 2 Austin, 4 from session 99), k6_loss picks the oracle in at least 8 and its mean regret is below k3_loss's and below every fixed candidate's.
Secondary, no pass mark: k8_loss against k6_loss on the 6 new cells.
Refuted if any part fails. Risks named in advance: 2020 is the COVID year (permits issued then ran long), so the test year itself may not follow the city split (H1) even if the selector does; for Y = 2019 the Y-6 and Y-8 cohorts are 2013 and 2011, and Seattle's older cohorts may be thin; for Y = 2021 and 2022 the 3-year cut has been chosen on those years (sessions 92 to 97), while 2019, 2020 and Austin-as-target were never looked at, so these cells are the cleaner test.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit f884eb495, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-17 04:09 to 04:32 UTC; row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) and dated completions (750,797, 108,259, 2,186,393) identical to sessions 95 to 99. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 04:33 to about 05:20 UTC, one process per cell at 1 thread (SF and Seattle cells first, then Austin). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_rep_i2c.json` (the six cells' files merged), `results/rounds_score_rep_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-rep results/rounds_score_rep_i2c.json`).
- Test q0.9 per new cell (days; nc3, nc5, stamp, stamp_len): SF 2019 83.31, 82.17, 82.53, 81.68; SF 2020 79.05, 77.97, 78.86, 78.22; Seattle 2019 82.25, 80.79, 81.09, 81.17; Seattle 2020 99.75, 95.31, 95.51, 96.32; Austin 2021 66.76, 83.93, 82.43, 83.35; Austin 2022 65.18, 81.25, 81.06, 82.33. Oracle: stamp_len, nc5, nc5, nc5, nc3, nc3.
- Target cohorts (completed by D of all): SF Y-6 33,348 and 34,499 rows (38%, 38% open), Seattle Y-6 6,681 and 7,352 (16%, 16%), Austin Y-6 62,496 and 66,570 (10%, 9%); Y-8 smaller by 2 to 22%.
- (H1, the city split) Refuted: in 2019 and 2020 a 5-year rule beats the 3-year cut in SF too (nc5 minus nc3 on the test year -1.14, -1.09; Seattle -1.46, -4.44). The SF preference for the 3-year cut that sessions 92 to 99 found is a property of test years 2021 and 2022, not of the city.
- (H2) Refuted: k6_loss picks nc3 in all 4 new SF/Seattle cells (12 of 12 seeds), 0 hits.
- (H3) Refuted: k6_loss's mean regret over those cells is 2.15 days (1.63, 1.08, 1.46, 4.44); the fixed nc5 rule's is 0.12.
- (H4) Refuted: k6_loss's mean test q0.9 there is 86.09, the worst of the fixed rules (nc5 84.06, stamp_len 84.35, stamp 84.50, nc3 86.09).
- (H5) Held: Austin as target, every candidate but nc3 loses 15.7 to 17.2 days and every selector picks nc3, regret 0.
- (H6, pooled) Refuted: k6_loss is the oracle in 6 of 10 cells (needed 8). Its mean regret, 0.86 days, is below k3_loss's (1.80) and every fixed rule's (nc3 1.80, nc5 3.83, stamp 4.10, stamp_len 4.36), so that part held.
- Secondary: k8_loss picks nc3 in all 6 new cells too, the same picks and regret as k6_loss. No selector of the six (k3, k5, k6, k8 plain; k3, k6 IPCW) departs from nc3 in a new cell except k3_ipcw once (Seattle 2019, stamp_len, regret 0.38).
- The score gap: nc5 minus nc3 on the Y-6 score is +7.41 and +4.06 in SF, +2.22 and +1.53 in Seattle, against -0.82 and -0.92 in session 99's Seattle cells. On the Y-6 cohorts of 2013 and 2014 the 3-year cut scores better in both cities, and on the test years 2019 and 2020 it loses in both.
- What this says (post hoc): session 99's 4 of 4 was the selector agreeing with a city split that holds only in 2021 and 2022, the years on which the candidate rules were found. Outside them the target-cohort score behaves like "always take the 3-year cut": the old completed rows still reward the shorter tail, and only Seattle's 2015 and 2016 cohorts flipped. Pooled, k6_loss leaves less regret than any fixed rule only because Austin as target punishes the 5-year rules by 16 days; that is one city where every selector, and the fixed 3-year cut, agree. No selector tested here can be recommended over a fixed rule; the deployable advice is now "a 5-year cut for SF and Seattle in most years, a 3-year cut when the target looks like Austin", and choosing between those still needs a signal this programme has not found.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; per-row predictions not saved, so no paired bootstrap; the 2020 test year is the COVID year; 10 cells is still few and 6 of them share the same pick, so the pooled regret is dominated by the 2 Austin cells; the candidate rules were found on 2021 and 2022 (sessions 92 to 97).
Next: (1) why Austin as target wants the 3-year cut by 16 days (its sources are only SF and Seattle; the 5-year rules keep their long open permits, which Austin's short builds never have), and whether a selector on source-target build-length overlap (the target's own median build of its Y-3 completed rows against each source's) picks the cut; (2) save per-row predictions so a paired bootstrap of the selector's score difference is possible; (3) test years 2017 and 2018, to see whether 2021 and 2022 or 2019 and 2020 are the unusual pair.

## Session 99, 2026-11-16: the target city's own six-year-old permits pick the right source rule in all 4 city-years: scored on its Y-6 permits completed by D, the selector picks the 3-year cut in SF and the 5-year cut in Seattle (the per-cell oracle each time, regret 0, mean test q0.9 71.12 days against the best fixed rule's 72.27); the Y-5 cohort gets 3 of 4 (regret 0.99) and IPCW on the Y-3 rows narrows the gap but never crosses it; F0, F1, F2, F3, F5 held, F4 refuted

Why: session 98 found the two scores a deployment can compute on its own Y-3 permits biased in opposite directions: the rows completed by D are cut at about three years of follow-up, so they reward the 3-year cut's shorter tail in both cities (right in SF, 5.46 and 3.95 days of regret in Seattle); the censored loss rewards the longest tail everywhere. Its "Next" named two fixes: score on older target cohorts, whose completed rows are truncated only past six years, and reweight the Y-3 completed rows by the inverse chance of being seen by D (IPCW).
- Method: `rounds.py` with `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1` now also predicts the held-out city's Y-5 and Y-6 permits (`TGT_KS` = 3, 5, 6) and reports, per cohort k, `tgt_k[k].loss` (q0.9 pinball on its rows completed by D) and `tgt_k[k].ipcw` (the same, weighted by 1/G(y) from `transfer.ipcw_completed` on that city-year's own issue days, floor 0.05). The target rows are never trained on. `rounds.py x --summarise-tfol FILE` (`target_follow_summary`) picks, per cell and per selector (k3, k5, k6, plain and IPCW), the candidate with the lowest score and scores the pick on the test year.
Design (fixed before any model run): session 98's design unchanged (cascade2 class; candidates nc3, nc5, nc5_stamp, nc5_stamp_len; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; q0.9 only; fresh pull 2026-11-16; one process per cell at 1 thread), output `results/rounds_score_tfol_i2c.json`. Primary selector k6_loss (`TFOL_PRIMARY`): the oldest cohort, unweighted, so no weighting assumption enters.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (F0, check) Every refit's test q0.9 and its k3_loss equal session 98's test q0.9 and tgt_loss within 0.05 days (`results/rounds_score_tsel_i2c.json`).
- (F1) The primary selector (k6_loss) picks nc3 in both SF cells and a 5-year rule (nc5, stamp or stamp_len) in both Seattle cells.
- (F2) The primary selector's mean test q0.9 over the 4 cells is lower than every fixed candidate's (session 98: best fixed nc5, 72.27).
- (F3) In both Seattle cells, nc5 minus nc3 on k6_loss is lower than on k3_loss (longer follow-up moves the score toward the long tail).
- (F4) The IPCW Y-3 selector (k3_ipcw) picks a 5-year rule in at least one Seattle cell.
- (F5) The primary selector's mean regret against the per-cell oracle is at most 1.0 day.
Refuted if any part fails. Risks named in advance: the Y-6 cohort (2015, 2016) predates the test years by six years, so cohort drift (pre-COVID permits, other mixes) may swamp the city signal; IPCW weights are floored at 0.05 and the 3-year follow-up leaves no rows past about 1,100 days to reweight, so IPCW may not move the Y-3 score toward the long tail at all.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit bbeed5aa1, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-16 23:08 to 23:22 UTC; row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) and dated completions (750,797, 108,259, 2,186,393) identical to sessions 95 to 98. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 23:23 UTC to 00:00 UTC on 17 November, one process per cell at 1 thread. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_tfol_i2c.json` (the four cells' files merged), `results/rounds_score_tfol_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-tfol results/rounds_score_tfol_i2c.json`).
- Target cohorts (rows, open at D): SF Y-3 40,357 and 40,693 (45%, 46% open), Y-5 37,119 and 38,080 (40%, 42%), Y-6 37,230 and 37,119 (40%, 40%); Seattle Y-3 7,567 and 7,824 (30%), Y-5 8,042 and 8,048 (18%, 25%), Y-6 7,572 and 8,042 (16%, 17%).
- (F0, check) Held: all 16 refits' test q0.9 and their k3_loss equal session 98's to every printed digit (difference 0.000).
- (F1) Held: k6_loss picks nc3 in both SF cells and nc5 in both Seattle cells. Per seed, 12 of 12 picks are on the right side of the city split (SF nc3 6 of 6; Seattle nc5 4, stamp 1, stamp_len 1).
- Scores (k6_loss, days; nc3, nc5, stamp, stamp_len): SF 2021 60.92, 65.73, 66.73, 65.23; SF 2022 70.00, 71.55, 72.65, 72.14; Seattle 2021 63.69, 62.87, 64.05, 63.73; Seattle 2022 65.99, 65.07, 65.69, 65.74.
- (F2) Held: the primary selector's mean test q0.9 is 71.12, below every fixed rule (nc3 73.47, nc5 72.27, stamp 72.91, stamp_len 73.18): 1.15 days better than the best fixed rule.
- (F3) Held: nc5 minus nc3 on the Seattle target score falls from +4.39 and +2.62 (Y-3) to -0.82 and -0.92 (Y-6). In SF it narrows too (+8.55, +5.25 to +4.82, +1.55) but keeps its sign.
- (F4) Refuted: k3_ipcw picks nc3 in both Seattle cells. The weights shrink nc5 minus nc3 (Seattle +4.39 to +3.16, +2.62 to +1.03; SF +8.55 to +7.10, +5.25 to +2.55) but never flip it: with three years of follow-up there are no rows past the 3-year cut's horizon to reweight.
- (F5) Held: k6_loss's mean regret is 0 (the oracle in every cell); k5_loss's is 0.99 (it picks nc3 in Seattle 2022, nc5 minus nc3 +2.07, and nc5 in Seattle 2021, -0.45, 1 of 3 seeds nc3).
- What this says (post hoc): the 3-year and 5-year rules differ in how long a tail they predict, so a target score can only tell them apart on rows whose follow-up reaches past three years. The Y-3 completed rows cannot (session 98), IPCW cannot create rows that are not there, the Y-5 cohort is borderline and the Y-6 cohort does it in all four cells. A deployment on D can compute this score from its own records: permits issued six years earlier and completed by D. The Seattle margins are small (0.8 to 0.9 days on 6,300 to 6,600 completed rows, against an SF margin of 1.6 to 4.8), and one year less of follow-up flips one Seattle cell, so the rule is "score on the oldest cohort with full follow-up past the longer cut", not a margin to bank on.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; two target cities and two test years (4 cells), so a 4-of-4 hit is suggestive, not a test of significance (a coin picks it 1 time in 16); the Y-6 cohorts are 2015 and 2016, before COVID, and the score could lose the city signal in a period of faster drift; per-row predictions not saved, so no paired bootstrap of the Y-6 score difference; Austin not held out; the selector chooses among four rules fixed by earlier sessions, which were themselves found on these test years (sessions 92 to 97), so the fixed-rule comparison favours the fixed rules if anything.
Next: (1) a paired bootstrap over the target's Y-6 rows of the nc5 minus nc3 score, to say how often the pick would flip on resampled permits; (2) more cells: Austin held out, and test years 2019 and 2020 (Y-6 = 2013, 2014), to see whether 4 of 4 survives; (3) a continuous version: score the 2-, 3-, 5- and 8-year cuts (session 92's grid) on Y-6 and Y-8 and pick the cut, not just the side.
