## Session 98, 2026-11-16: the target city's own Y-3 history cannot pick the source rule yet: scored on its permits completed by D it picks the 3-year cut in all 4 city-years (right in SF, 5.46 and 3.95 days of regret in Seattle, mean 2.35), scored by the censored loss it picks the 5-year stamp rule in all 4 (mean regret 1.79); the fixed 5-year cut (72.27) beats both; every refit reproduced its earlier fit to the digit; G0, G4 held, G1, G2, G3 refuted

Why: three sessions (92, 96, 97) show the source rows that help one target hurt the other: the 3-year staleness cut and the thinned long tail help San Francisco, the 5-year cut and the kept long closures help Seattle. Session 92 tried to pick the cut from the sources' own Y-3 rows by a censored loss and failed (that score rewards a longer tail whatever the target needs). Sessions 96 and 97 asked for a target-chosen rule. A deployment on D = 1 January Y does hold some of the target's own history: its Y-3 permits, some completed by D. This run scores each candidate source rule on those rows and keeps the best, without ever training on them.
- Method: `rounds.py` with `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1`: every cpin_es3 variant also predicts the held-out city's Y-3 permits and reports `tgt_loss` (q0.9 pinball on those completed by D) and `tgt_closs` (censored loss on all of them, open ones with their window at D). `rounds.py x --summarise-tsel FILE` (`target_sel_summary`) picks, per cell, the candidate with the lowest `tgt_loss` (primary selector) and the lowest `tgt_closs` (second selector), and scores each pick on the test year against every fixed rule and the per-cell oracle.
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; candidates nc3 (3-year cut), nc5 (5-year cut), nc5_stamp (session 96's practical rule), nc5_stamp_len (session 97's length-thinned tail); H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; q0.9 only (`CPIN_TAUS=0.9`); fresh pull 2026-11-16; `CPIN_TARGET_SEL=1 rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --status-dir DIR_STATUS --variants cpin_es3_nc3 cpin_es3_nc5 cpin_es3_nc5_stamp cpin_es3_nc5_stamp_len --held sf|seattle --test-years Y --out-name rounds_score_tsel_<cell>.json`, one process per cell at 1 thread, merged into `results/rounds_score_tsel_i2c.json`. All four candidates are refit here (the target scores need the models).
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (G0, check) Every refit's test q0.9 equals its earlier fit within 0.05 days (nc3: session 92, `rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`; nc5: session 95; stamp, stamp_len: session 97).
- (G1) The primary selector picks nc3 in both SF cells and a 5-year rule (nc5, stamp or stamp_len) in both Seattle cells.
- (G2) The selected rule's mean test q0.9 over the 4 cells is lower than every fixed candidate's.
- (G3) The primary selector's mean regret against the per-cell oracle is at most 0.5 days.
- (G4) The censored selector picks a 5-year rule in all 4 cells (it rewards the longer tail, as in session 92).
Refuted if any part fails. A risk named in advance: the target's Y-3 rows completed by D are truncated at about 3 years, so the primary score may favour the shorter tail (nc3) everywhere, which would refute G1 in Seattle.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit cfae7dfc8, before any model run. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 18:28 to about 19:00 UTC, one process per cell at 1 thread. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_tsel_i2c.json` (the four cells' files merged), `results/rounds_score_tsel_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-tsel results/rounds_score_tsel_i2c.json`).
- Test q0.9 per cell (days; nc3, nc5, stamp, stamp_len): SF 2021 70.79, 72.35, 72.77, 72.29. SF 2022 58.16, 61.21, 62.28, 61.64. Seattle 2021 91.07, 85.61, 86.60, 88.07. Seattle 2022 73.86, 69.91, 70.00, 70.73. Oracle: nc3 in SF, nc5 in Seattle.
- Target selection set: the held city's Y-3 permits, 40,357 and 40,693 in SF (45% open at D), 7,567 and 7,824 in Seattle (30% open).
- (G0, check) Held: all 16 refits equal their earlier fits (sessions 92, 95, 97) to every printed digit.
- (G1) Refuted: the completed-row selector picks nc3 in all 4 cells, and in all 12 per-seed picks. Its score ranks nc3 lowest everywhere (nc5 minus nc3 on the target rows: SF +8.55, +5.25; Seattle +4.39, +2.62), while on the test year nc5 beats nc3 in Seattle by 5.46 and 3.95 days. The gap is narrower in Seattle, in the right direction, but never crosses.
- (G2) Refuted: the selected rule's mean is 73.47, the worst of the four fixed rules' means (nc3 73.47, nc5 72.27, stamp 72.91, stamp_len 73.18); the censored selector's is 72.91.
- (G3) Refuted: mean regret 2.35 days (0, 0, 5.46, 3.95).
- (G4) Held: the censored selector picks nc5_stamp in all 4 cells (regret 1.98, 4.12, 0.99, 0.09; mean 1.79). Its score falls with every long prediction (nc5 minus nc3: -35 to -71 in SF, -24 in Seattle), as session 92 found on the source rows.
- What this says (post hoc): the two scores a deployment can compute on its own Y-3 permits are biased in opposite directions and neither carries the city signal. The rows completed by D are cut at three years of follow-up, so they reward the short tail; the censored loss counts every open permit as at least its window, and many never close, so it rewards the long tail. Each picks one rule for both cities. Until a selector can tell the cities apart, a fixed 5-year cut (with or without the stamp drop) is the safer deployment default: mean 72.27 against 73.47 for the 3-year cut, and its worst cell is +3.05 days against nc3's +5.46.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; two target cities, two years; the target's Y-3 rows are one selection year; per-row predictions were not saved, so a reweighted score (IPCW on the target rows) cannot be computed from these files.
Next: a selection set with longer follow-up: score the candidates on the target's Y-5 or Y-6 permits (five or six years old on D, so the truncation that sank G1 bites only past the 3-year cut's own horizon), and an IPCW-weighted version of the completed-row score on Y-3 (weights from the target's own issue-to-D windows, as `transfer.ipcw_completed`); save per-row predictions so both can be scored from one fit.

## Session 97, 2026-11-16: length and class, about half each in Seattle: dropping as many live permits of the same lengths as the non-stamp dead close-outs costs Seattle +1.47 and +0.73 days at q0.9 (the closures themselves +2.52 and +1.75) and helps San Francisco in 6 of 6 seeds (-0.48, -0.64; closures -0.28, -1.25), so most of the effect is the label mass in the long tail, not the close-out class; the closures over 365 days are 40 to 44% of them and carry 64 to 75% of Seattle's cost; L0, L1, L3 held, L2, L4, L5 refuted

Why: session 96 found that dropping the non-stamp dead close-outs (mostly Austin's withdrawn permits, long tail: dead p90 1,080 days against done's 379) costs Seattle +1.75 and +2.52 days at q0.9 (worse in 6 of 6 seeds) and helps SF (better in 5 of 6), and read that post hoc as "long rows look like Seattle's own tail". Its "Next" asked whether the closures matter for their length or their class. This run tests that with a length-matched placebo: drop the same number of live (not dead) completed rows with the same label distribution. If length is what matters, the placebo costs Seattle as much as the closures did; if class, it costs nothing. A second variant drops only the long closures (label over 365 days) to see whether they carry the cost.
- Method: `rounds.py` gains `len_drop` and two variants on top of session 96's stamp rule (the recommended practical rule): `cpin_es3_nc5_stamp_len` (stamp drop plus, per source city and per decile bin of its non-stamp dead labels completed by D, as many randomly chosen live rows completed by D in that bin; rng seed 97, fixed before any run; dead rows kept) and `cpin_es3_nc5_stamp_long` (stamp drop plus the non-stamp dead rows completed by D with label over 365 days). Both apply to the training and Y-3 early-stopping sets separately, as `dead_drop` does.
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; q0.9 only (`CPIN_TAUS=0.9`); fresh pull 2026-11-16; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --status-dir DIR_STATUS --variants cpin_es3_nc5_stamp cpin_es3_nc5_stamp_len cpin_es3_nc5_stamp_long --held sf|seattle --out-name rounds_score_len_i2c.json`, one process per cell at 1 thread. stamp is refit as a check; clean (session 95, `results/rounds_score_clean_i2c.json`) is reused if the pull's row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) and dead completion counts (3,130, 423, 172,473) equal session 95's, else refit in this run.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (L0, check) The stamp refit equals session 96's q0.9 within 0.05 days in all 4 cells.
- (L1) len_drop matches at least 99% of the non-stamp dead rows (shortfall under 1%) in every cell.
- (L2, length) In Seattle, stamp_len minus stamp is at least +1.0 day in both years.
- (L3) In SF, stamp_len minus stamp, mean over 2021 and 2022, is below 0.
- (L4) In Seattle, stamp_long minus stamp is at least 75% of clean minus stamp (session 96: +2.52 in 2021, +1.75 in 2022) in both years.
- (L5) stamp_long drops at most 40% of the non-stamp dead rows in every cell.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 6bbfc1c16, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-16 07:08 to 07:25 UTC; row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) and dead completion counts (3,130, 423, 172,473) identical to session 95's, so clean was reused, not refit. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 07:25 to about 08:05 UTC, one process per cell at 1 thread (`CPIN_THREADS=1`). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_len_i2c.json` (the four cells' files merged), `results/rounds_score_len_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-len results/rounds_score_len_i2c.json`).
- q0.9 per cell (days; stamp refit here, clean from session 95): SF 2021 stamp 72.77, stamp_len 72.29, stamp_long 72.62, clean 72.49. SF 2022 62.28, 61.64, 61.37, 61.03. Seattle 2021 86.60, 88.07, 88.50, 89.12. Seattle 2022 70.00, 70.73, 71.12, 71.75.
- (L0, check) Held: the stamp refit equals session 96's q0.9 to every printed digit in all 4 cells (72.77, 62.28, 86.60, 70.00).
- (L1) Held: len_drop matched all 27,622 to 31,133 non-stamp dead rows in every cell (shortfall 0).
- (L2, length) Refuted as written: in Seattle stamp_len minus stamp is +1.47 (2021) and +0.73 (2022), not at least +1.0 in both; per seed +0.64 to +2.34 in 2021, -0.04, +2.25, -0.02 in 2022. The placebo carries 58% and 42% of the closures' Seattle cost (+2.52, +1.75).
- (L3) Held: in SF stamp_len minus stamp is -0.48 and -0.64, mean -0.56, better in 6 of 6 seeds (-0.38 to -0.71); the closures themselves gave -0.28 and -1.25 (mean -0.77).
- (L4) Refuted, by a hair in one year: stamp_long minus stamp is +1.90 in Seattle 2021 (75% bound 1.89, held) and +1.12 in 2022 (bound 1.31, refuted); per seed -0.61 to +3.85. The long closures carry 75% and 64% of the closures' Seattle cost; in SF they give -0.15 and -0.91.
- (L5) Refuted: closures over 365 days are 40.4 to 44.0% of the non-stamp dead rows (12,167 to 12,579), not at most 40%.
- Mean q0.9 prediction on completed test rows: removing either set lowers it against stamp in every cell (SF 703 to 679 and 685, 703 to 678 and 676; Seattle 731 to 721 and 721, 718 to 712 and 712), so the dropped long rows were holding the borrowed tail up, and Seattle, whose builds run long, loses that.
- What this says (post hoc): session 96's reading, "long rows look like Seattle's own tail", is about half right. Removing the same label mass from live permits reproduces most of SF's gain and about half of Seattle's cost, so the effect is mostly the length distribution of what is removed, not the close-out class. The rest of Seattle's cost (about +1 day, +1.05 and +1.02 in the two years) is specific to the closures, and it is within the seed spread of a single cell, so this run cannot say whether it is real. For a deployment, the result strengthens session 96's rule: keep the non-stamp closures, since dropping them is equivalent to thinning the borrowed tail, which helps short-build targets and hurts long-build ones; any thinning of source rows by length is a per-target decision, like the staleness cut.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds and one placebo draw (rng seed 97); a different draw could move a cell by a few tenths; SF and Seattle held out, Austin not run; the length match is per source city and per decile bin, not per permit class; statuses as on the pull.
Next: the residual class effect needs more seeds or a paired bootstrap over test rows on Seattle (stamp_len against clean); and a target-chosen tail rule, since three sessions now show the source rows that help one target hurt the other: score candidate source thinnings (none, length-thinned, 3- or 5-year cut) on the target's own Y-3 completed rows and keep the best.

## Session 96, 2026-11-16: the 180-day stamps are 82 to 83% of the dead rows but not what costs Seattle: dropping only them moves q0.9 by +0.64 days on average, while the other 17% of closures (mostly Austin's withdrawn permits) carry Seattle's cost (+1.75 and +2.52 days, worse in 6 of 6 seeds) and help SF (better in 5 of 6); the city pattern of the staleness cut survives cleaning (3 years in SF, 5 in Seattle), and on the 3-year cut cleaning costs 0.68 days, worse in 11 of 12 seeds; T1, C1, C2 held, T2, T3 refuted

Why: session 95 dropped every dead permit's completion date from the borrowed model's training and found the rule removes 6.5 to 8.6% of rows, nearly all of them Austin's pre-2000 void and expired permits stamped exactly 180 days after issue, and that the drop costs Seattle 1.8 to 3.5 days at q0.9. Its "Next" asked to separate the stamp from the other closures, and to check whether the staleness cut tuned on stamped data (session 92: 3 years best in SF, 5 in Seattle) still holds once the sources are clean.
- Method: `rounds.py` gains `cpin_es3_nc5_stamp` (cpin_es3_nc5 dropping only the dead rows completed by D whose label is exactly `STAMP_DAYS` = 180) and `cpin_es3_nc3_clean` (session 95's dead drop on session 92's 3-year cut). Summary: `rounds.py x --summarise-stamp results/rounds_score_stamp_i2c.json`, against session 95's nc5 and clean (`results/rounds_score_clean_i2c.json`) and session 92's nc3 (`results/rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`).
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; q0.9 only (`CPIN_TAUS=0.9`, as session 95); fresh pull 2026-11-16; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --status-dir DIR_STATUS --variants cpin_es3_nc5_stamp cpin_es3_nc3_clean --held sf|seattle --out-name rounds_score_stamp_i2c.json`. nc5, clean and nc3 are not refit: sessions 93 and 95 reproduced those models to every digit on feeds with the same row counts. Reuse rule: if the fresh pull's row counts (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435) or dead completion counts (3,130, 423, 172,473) differ from session 95's, nc5 and clean are refit in this run too and the comparison uses the refits.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds on completed test rows, days):
- (T1) In every cell the stamp rule drops between 75% and 95% of the rows session 95's clean rule dropped.
- (T2) In Seattle, stamp minus nc5 is at least +1.0 day in both years (the stamps carry the cost session 95 found).
- (T3) In every cell clean and stamp differ by less than 1 day (the non-stamp closures barely move the tail).
- (C1) On clean sources the city pattern of the cut holds: nc3_clean beats nc5_clean in both SF cells, nc5_clean beats nc3_clean in both Seattle cells.
- (C2) nc3_clean minus nc3, mean over the 4 cells, lies within plus or minus 1.5 days.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 0343af3e2, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-16 03:40 to 03:48 UTC (status pull 03:40 to 03:43); row counts identical to session 95's (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435), and so are the dead completion counts (3,130, 423, 172,473), so the reuse rule held and nc5, clean and nc3 were not refit. Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 03:49 to 04:02 UTC, one process per cell at 1 thread (session 95 ran Seattle at 2). A thread check refit Seattle 2022 nc5 at 1 thread: its three seeds equal session 95's to every digit (69.716, 69.031, 70.972), so the thread count does not move these models and the reuse is safe. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_stamp_i2c.json`, `results/rounds_score_stamp_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-stamp results/rounds_score_stamp_i2c.json`).
- q0.9 per cell (days; nc5, clean from session 95, nc3 from session 92): SF 2021 nc5 72.35, stamp 72.77, clean 72.49, nc3 70.79, nc3_clean 71.02. SF 2022 61.21, 62.28, 61.03, 58.16, 58.95. Seattle 2021 85.61, 86.60, 89.12, 91.07, 91.76. Seattle 2022 69.91, 70.00, 71.75, 73.86, 74.89.
- (T1) Held: the stamp rule drops 139,261 to 139,269 rows, 81.7 to 83.4% of what the clean rule drops (band 75 to 95%), all Austin's but 8 SF rows with Seattle held out.
- (T2) Refuted: in Seattle stamp minus nc5 is +0.99 (2021) and +0.09 (2022), not at least +1.0 in both; per seed -0.62 to +2.92. Over the 4 cells stamp minus nc5 averages +0.64; SF 2022 is worse in all 3 seeds (+0.75 to +1.38).
- (T3) Refuted: clean minus stamp is -0.28 (SF 2021), -1.25 (SF 2022), +2.52 (Seattle 2021) and +1.75 (Seattle 2022); per seed Seattle is worse in 6 of 6 (+0.41 to +3.79) and SF better in 5 of 6 (-2.09 to +0.21). The 27,600 to 31,100 non-stamp dead rows (mostly Austin's withdrawn permits with a long tail, session 94) are what session 95's Seattle cost came from, and they help SF.
- (C1) Held: on clean sources nc3_clean beats nc5_clean in SF (71.02 against 72.49, 58.95 against 61.03) and loses in Seattle (91.76 against 89.12, 74.89 against 71.75), the same pattern session 92 found on stamped data.
- (C2) Held: nc3_clean minus nc3 averages +0.68 days (band within 1.5); per cell +0.23, +0.79, +0.69, +1.03, worse in 11 of 12 seeds (-0.03 to +1.36). Cleaning costs at the 3-year cut too, a little.
- Mean q0.9 prediction on completed test rows: the stamp-only model predicts the highest tail in 3 of 4 cells (SF 703 and 703 against nc5's 684 and 681; Seattle 731 and 718 against 711 and 709); dropping the other closures as well brings SF back down (689, 675). nc3_clean predicts 550 to 601, far below nc5's family, as nc3 does.
- Censored score over all test rows (post hoc; never-completed rows as lower bounds at the pull): stamp is the lowest of nc5, clean and stamp in all 4 cells (SF 320.5 against nc5 326.3 and clean 323.9; 238.0 against 243.4, 243.8; Seattle 350.3 against 354.4, 353.2; 263.7 against 265.7, 263.8). Session 92 showed this score rewards a longer tail whatever the completed rows say, so it does not rank the models.
- What this says (post hoc): of session 95's two explanations, the "short-duration anchor" one is wrong for Seattle. The 180-day stamps are four-fifths of the dropped rows, yet dropping them alone costs Seattle about what seed noise does. The cost sits in the fifth that are real-looking close-outs with long durations (Austin's dead p90 1,080 days): to a Seattle target, whose builds run long, those long rows look like its own tail, and the model that keeps them predicts it better. SF, whose builds are shorter, is helped by losing them. So the closures are a city-dependent signal, like the staleness cut, not noise to scrub. For a deployable model the practical rule is narrow: drop the 180-day stamps (a labelling artefact, +0.64 days on average, within seed noise in 3 of 4 cells) and keep the other dead close-outs; the cut stays per city (3 years for SF, 5 for Seattle) whether or not the sources are cleaned.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds, so per-cell differences under about 1 day are inside seed spread; SF and Seattle held out, Austin not run; statuses as on the pull, not on D; the 180-day stamp is inferred from the label distribution, not from Austin's documentation; stamp and nc3_clean were run at a different thread count from the reused models, checked on one cell.
Next: whether the long non-stamp closures help Seattle because of their length or their class (rerun with only Austin's withdrawn closures dropped, then with them reweighted); and a city-selected cut (3 or 5 years) chosen from the target's own Y-3 completed rows, since a censored source score cannot pick it (session 92).

## Session 95, 2026-11-16: dropping the dead permits' completion dates from the borrowed model's training costs Seattle 1.8 to 3.5 days at q0.9 and leaves San Francisco unchanged (mean +1.33); the rule drops 6.5 to 8.6% of training rows, not 1.5%, because 96% of Austin's pre-2000 void and expired permits carry a completion date exactly 180 days after issue, the most common build-time label in Austin's feed (143,212 rows); K4, K5 held, K1, K2, K3 refuted

Why: session 94 found that 0.04% (SF), 0.40% (Seattle) and 1.49% (Austin) of dated completions belong to permits the status calls dead (Austin's mostly "withdrawn"), with a long tail (Austin's dead p90 1,080 days against done's 379). Every model here reads the completion date as a build's end. Its "Next" asked to refit cpin_es3_nc5 with those rows dropped and measure what the closures cost at q0.9.
- Method: `src/status_pull.py` pulls permit id, completion day and status (Seattle also session 94's "Closed as Incomplete" marker, computed by the API) for every permit with a completion date, to `DATA_DIR/<city>_status.csv`. `rounds.py` joins it on (permit id, completion day) (`attach_status`) and adds variant `cpin_es3_nc5_clean`: cpin_es3_nc5 with the source rows completed by D whose status is dead dropped from training and the Y-3 early-stopping set (`dead_drop`); a dead permit completed after D stays as an open row, since on D it is open. With `--status-dir` every cpin variant is also scored on the live test rows (completed test-year rows whose status is not dead) and reports its mean q0.9 prediction on completed test rows.
- Leak disclosed: the status is the status on the pull day, not on D. For a row completed before D the close-out status is set at close-out, so it was almost surely the same on D; this is assumed, not checked (the feeds keep no status history).
Design (fixed before any model run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull 2026-11-16; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --status-dir DIR_STATUS --variants cpin_es3_nc5 cpin_es3_nc5_clean --held sf|seattle --out-name rounds_score_clean_i2c.json`. Both variants are refit on the same pull, so the comparison does not depend on how the feeds moved since session 91.
Predictions (q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss over 3 seeds, days):
- (K1) In every cell the clean rule drops between 0.5% and 3% of the training plus early-stopping rows, and at least 80% of the dropped rows are Austin's.
- (K2) clean minus nc5 at q0.9 on all completed test rows, mean over the 4 cells, lies within plus or minus 1 day.
- (K3) clean's mean q0.9 prediction on completed test rows is lower than nc5's in all 4 cells.
- (K4) On the live test rows, clean minus nc5 at q0.9 is no larger than the same difference on all completed test rows in at least 3 of the 4 cells.
- (K5, check) nc5's q0.9 test loss on this pull is within 0.5 days of session 91's (`results/rounds_score_nc_i2c.json`) in all 4 cells.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Pre-registration commit 11fb2ef59, before any model run. Pull 2026-11-16 01:40 to 01:54 UTC, row counts identical to session 91's (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435); status pull 01:40 to 01:47 (`src/status_pull.py`: SF 750,797, Seattle 108,259, Austin 2,186,393 dated completions; dead 3,130, 423 and 172,473). Runs on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 02:12 to about 03:20 UTC: SF both years in one process (1 thread), Seattle 2021 and 2022 in one process each (2 threads), Austin held out added after launch on the core SF freed (1 thread; reported, not tested). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_clean_i2c.json` (SF and Seattle), `results/rounds_score_clean_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-clean results/rounds_score_clean_i2c.json`), `results/rounds_score_clean_austin_i2c.json`, `results/stamp_check.json` (`src/stamp_check.py DATA_DIR STATUS_DIR`).
- Deviation from the design: only the q0.9 model was fitted (`CPIN_TAUS=0.9`). A first launch with q0.5 as well ran 442 to 516 s per q0.5 fit at 2 threads, which would have taken over 3 hours; no prediction reads q0.5, so it was stopped after 18 minutes, before any cell finished, and relaunched.
- (K5, check) Held: nc5's q0.9 test loss equals session 91's to every digit in all 4 cells (SF 2021 72.35, SF 2022 61.21, Seattle 2021 85.61, Seattle 2022 69.91), and Austin's equals session 92's (83.93, 81.25).
- (K1) Refuted: the rule drops 166,883 to 170,402 rows, 6.5 to 8.6% of training plus early-stopping rows (band 0.5 to 3%); 98.2 to 99.8% of them are Austin's (band at least 80%, held). Session 94 counted issue years 2010 to 2022 only; the training set reaches back to the 1980s, and Austin's permits issued before 2000 are 16.3% dead with a completion date (140,722 of the 860,853 whose status is done or dead).
- What those rows are (post hoc, `src/stamp_check.py`, `results/stamp_check.json`): 135,567 of the 140,722 (96.3%) are exactly 180 days from issue to "completion", and so are 20% of Austin's 2000 to 2009 dead completions; after 2010 it is 0.3%. Among Austin's done permits 0.2% sit on 180 days in every era. 180 days is the most common build-time label in Austin's whole feed (143,212 of 2,182,859 dated completions, 6.6%; next 1 day, 53,965). SF's and Seattle's dead completions show no such spike (at most 0.5% on 180 days). So before about 2005 Austin wrote the expiry date of a void or expired permit into its completion field: a stamp, not an event.
- (K2) Refuted: clean minus nc5 at q0.9 on completed test rows, mean over 4 cells +1.33 days (band within 1). Per cell: SF 2021 +0.14, SF 2022 -0.18, Seattle 2021 +3.51, Seattle 2022 +1.84. Per seed, Seattle is worse in all 6 (+0.92 to +4.01); SF's seeds straddle zero (-0.79 to +1.33).
- (K3) Refuted: clean's mean q0.9 prediction on completed test rows is higher, not lower, in 3 of 4 cells (SF 2021 688.9 against 683.9 days, Seattle 2021 730.0 against 710.8, Seattle 2022 728.6 against 709.1; lower only in SF 2022, 674.7 against 680.9). Dropping a mass of 180-day labels, far below the 0.9 quantile, moves the quantile up.
- (K4) Held, but uninformative: the test years hold 0 to 33 dead completions (0 to 0.7%), so live and all-row differences agree to 0.03 days.
- Rounds: Seattle's clean tail models run about twice as many rounds (mean 226 against 119 in 2021, 157 against 68 in 2022); SF's do not move consistently (100 against 61, 33 against 69).
- Censored score over all test rows (never-completed ones as lower bounds at the pull): clean is lower in 3 of 4 cells (SF 2021 323.9 against 326.3, Seattle 2021 353.2 against 354.4, Seattle 2022 263.8 against 265.7; SF 2022 243.8 against 243.4), as the longer tail predicts: the same pull between completed-row and censored scores session 92 found.
- Austin held out (reported, not tested; sources SF and Seattle, 3,260 to 3,295 rows dropped): -5.73 days in 2021 and +0.89 in 2022, per seed -10.4 to +3.3; its tail models stop after 3 to 5 rounds, so these cells are seed noise more than signal.
- What this says (post hoc): the dead permits' completion dates are not what holds the deployable borrowed model back. Session 94's 1.5% was the post-2010 share; over the whole training history it is 7 to 9% of rows, nearly all Austin's pre-2000 180-day stamps. Dropping them is the right data hygiene for anyone reading Austin's feed as build times, and it lengthens the borrowed tail, but on completed test rows that costs Seattle 1.8 to 3.5 days and buys SF nothing. The stamped rows were acting as a short-duration anchor that kept the tail models from overfitting the long builds; without it they run longer and predict higher. Whether a stamp-free model needs its own early-stopping or staleness setting (the nc5 cut was tuned with the stamps in) is open.
- Limits: q0.9 only; 3 seeds; SF and Seattle tested, Austin reported; statuses as on the pull, not on D (for rows completed decades before D this is safe); the stamp is inferred from the label distribution, not from Austin's documentation, which this run did not find.
Next: rerun session 92's staleness sweep (2, 3, 5 years) on the stamp-free sources, to see whether the cut that suited the stamped data still suits the clean data; and drop only the 180-day stamped dead rows (not every dead row) to separate the stamp from the closures.

## Session 94, 2026-11-16: the completion dates are builds, not closures (0.04% of dated completions in SF, 0.4% in Seattle and 1.5% in Austin belong to permits the status calls dead), but 71% of Seattle's undated "Completed" permits issued 2010 to 2022 say "Closed as Incomplete" in their description, so session 93's "about 10,000 finished builds with a missing date" is wrong: at most 1,574 remain, and nothing in the feed dates them; V2, V4 held, V1, V3, V5 refuted

Why: session 93 read Seattle's permits with status "Completed" and no completion date as finished builds missing a date, and asked whether another field could date them. While listing the feed's columns this run saw three such permits (issued 2005 to 2006), and all three descriptions open "Closed as Incomplete - Expired Permit". If that is general, those permits never close, and the reverse question matters more: how many completion dates in each feed belong to permits the status calls dead (session 93's `closeout_status.json` shows Austin's dated completions 4.6% "void" and 2.5% "expired"), since that date is the issue-to-completion label every model here trains and scores on.
- Method: `src/label_audit.py` pulls status, issue date, completion date and permit type for SF, Seattle and Austin permits issued 2010 to 2022 (Seattle also a boolean, description contains "CLOSED AS INCOMPLETE", computed by the API; no text stored). Status groups: done (complete, completed, final, inspections completed, approved to occupy), dead (expired, void, withdrawn, cancelled, revoked, aborted, disapproved, denied, rejected, or Seattle's marker), other. Writes `results/label_audit.json`.
Predictions (written before any pull; issue years 2010 to 2022 unless said):
- (V1) At least 80% of Seattle's undated "Completed" permits carry the closed-as-incomplete marker.
- (V2) Fewer than 2% of Seattle's dated completions carry it.
- (V3) In Austin, dated completions in the dead group have a median issued-to-completed time more than twice the done group's.
- (V4) In each city the dead group is under 10% of dated completions.
- (V5) Over issue years 2010 to 2020, the dead share differs by more than 20 points between the highest and lowest permit type with at least 1,000 permits, in both Seattle and Austin.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Pull 2026-11-16, about 12 minutes (`/usr/bin/python3.13 src/label_audit.py`): SF 420,714, Seattle 88,626, Austin 836,092 permits issued 2010 to 2022. Output `results/label_audit.json` (counts only). Pre-registration commit 8c93ade23, before the pull.
- Dated completions by group (n, share, median and p90 days issued to completed): SF done 264,259 (99.86%, 123, 576), dead 117 (0.04%, 169, 1,581). Seattle done 69,786 (98.75%, 247, 691), dead 282 (0.40%, 406.5, 1,048), other 598 (0.85%, mostly "closed"). Austin done 741,169 (98.49%, 102, 379), dead 11,211 (1.49%, 140, 1,080; 10,625 of them "withdrawn" with a completion date), other 179.
- Undated permits: SF 156,059, of which 15,239 dead (9.8%) and 140,820 other (139,926 "issued"). Seattle 17,951, of which 14,971 dead (83%, the marker included), 1,594 done, 1,386 other. Austin 82,865, of which 82,063 dead (99%).
- Seattle's marker: 3,876 of the 5,450 undated "Completed" permits (71.1%); 36 of 70,666 dated completions (0.05%).
- (V1) Refuted: 71.1%, not at least 80%.
- (V2) Held: 0.05%.
- (V3) Refuted: Austin's dead median is 140 days against done's 102, 1.37 times, not more than 2. The tail differs more (p90 1,080 against 379).
- (V4) Held: 0.04%, 0.40% and 1.49% of dated completions.
- (V5) Refuted. Dead share over 2010 to 2020 by permit type (n >= 1,000): Seattle 7.9% (Demolition) to 17.6% (Addition/Alteration) among named types, 36.2% for the blank type (1,174 permits), so it held as written only through the blank type; Austin 6.1% (Driveway / Sidewalks) to 16.5% (Building Permit), 10.4 points. SF's types run 2.8 to 6.8% dead, but 12 to 48% of each still read "issued".
- The 1,574 undated "Completed" permits without the marker: 27 to 256 per issue year, more after 2015; a sample of 15 reads as real work (new houses, additions, an antenna). The feed's only other date on them is `expiresdate`, the permit's expiry, which does not date a completion. They can be returned at most as completed at an unknown time before the pull (interval-censored), not as labelled rows.
- What this says (post hoc): the issue-to-completion labels every model since session 1 used are clean enough: closures sit in the tail (Austin's dead p90 is 2.8 times done's), but at 1.5% at most they can move a 0.9 quantile only a little; whether they do is untested. The never-completed side is where the labels differ by feed. In Seattle and Austin 83 to 99% of undated permits are dead on the pull date, so a cure fraction can be learned there; within a city it varies by permit type by about 10 points, less than this run predicted, so a per-type cure weight adds less than a per-city one. In SF 90% of undated permits still read "issued", so SF gives no cure labels and needs one borrowed from the other two.
- Limits: statuses as on 2026-11-16, not on any decision date; one marker phrase, matched anywhere in the description (other closure wordings would be missed); the feeds' own permit types, not the cascade2 class; no model refit.
Next: refit cpin_es3_nc5 with the dead-with-date rows dropped from training (Austin mainly) to measure what the closures cost at q0.9; then the cure-fraction weight per source city, from permits issued at least 3 years before D whose status has settled, borrowed by SF.

## Session 93, 2026-11-15: a cut per source at its own 99th-percentile build time (Austin and SF about 3.2 years, Seattle 6.4) averages +0.58 days at q0.9, between session 92's best fixed cuts (3 years in SF, 5 in Seattle) and no better than 5 years overall; an independent rerun reproduces session 92's 2-, 3- and 8-year models to the digit; Seattle's and Austin's status fields label their never-completed permits (expired, void, and in Seattle "completed" with no date) where SF's mostly read "issued"; S1, S2, S4, S5 held, S3 refuted

Why: session 91's "Next" asked for a staleness sweep, a deployable way to set the cut, and the status fields of Seattle and Austin. This run and session 92 (PR #16320) started on the sweep in the same minutes; session 92 landed first. This run's design and predictions were committed before any model ran (commit e0e53171f, then labelled "session 92"), so they are reported here unchanged as session 93. What it adds over session 92: a per-source threshold rule (session 92 swept fixed ones and found a censored Y-3 score cannot pick among them), the status-field query for all three cities (session 92's "Next" (b)), and a replication.
- Method: `rounds.py` variant `cpin_es3_ncq`: as session 91's cpin_es3_nc5, with a threshold per source city, the 0.99 quantile of its own issue-to-completion days over its builds of years <= Y-2 completed by D (`rounds.q_threshold`), something a deployment on D can compute. The run also refit nc2, nc3 (1,096 days here, 1,095 in session 92) and nc8 as written in the pre-registration. `src/closeout_status.py` asks each feed's status field what its never-completed permits are (SoQL group-by).
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull 2026-11-15; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --variants cpin_es3_ncq cpin_es3_nc3 cpin_es3_nc2 cpin_es3_nc8 --held sf seattle`; compared with session 91's cpin_es3 and cpin_es3_nc5.
Predictions (as committed before any model run; q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss change against session 88's full on completed test-year rows, 3 seeds):
- (S1) cpin_es3_nc8's SF and Seattle q0.9 mean change is more than 5 days above cpin_es3_nc5's (-0.41).
- (S2) cpin_es3_nc2's SF q0.9 mean change (2 cells) is below cpin_es3_nc5's (+2.78).
- (S3) cpin_es3_ncq's per-source threshold lies between 2 and 5 years (730 to 1,826 days) for every source city-year, and its SF and Seattle q0.9 mean change is at most 0.5 days above cpin_es3_nc5's.
- (S4) No one fixed threshold of 2, 3, 5 and 8 years has the lowest q0.9 loss in all 4 city-years.
- (S5, check) The pull's SF, Seattle and Austin row counts are within 0.1% of session 91's (1,298,381; 194,311; 2,385,435).
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Pull 2026-11-15 19:39 to 19:55 UTC; one process, `/usr/bin/python3.13`, 4 threads, 19:55 to 22:38 UTC (about 10 minutes per variant and city-year). Outputs: `results/rounds_score_ncq_i2c.json` (ncq and the rerun nc2, nc3, nc8; per-seed losses kept), `results/rounds_score_ncq_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-ncq results/rounds_score_ncq_i2c.json`, which reads session 92's `rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json` and session 91's `rounds_score_nc_i2c.json`), `results/closeout_status.json` (`src/closeout_status.py`).
- (S5, check) Held: identical row counts to session 91's pull.
- Replication: this run's nc2, nc3 and nc8 q0.9 test losses equal session 92's to every digit in all 12 cells (largest difference 0.0), though the two runs were separate processes on separate containers at different thread counts (4 here, 2 there).
- cascade2's q0.9 test-loss change against full, completed test rows, days (cpin_es3 / nc2 / nc3 / nc5 / nc8 / ncq): SF 2021 +0.86 / +3.54 / +0.90 / +2.46 / +19.09 / -0.34. SF 2022 +0.23 / +1.19 / +0.05 / +3.10 / +21.73 / +1.98. Seattle 2021 +2.94 / +5.53 / +1.50 / -3.95 / -0.98 / +0.66. Seattle 2022 +2.48 / +1.68 / +0.73 / -3.22 / -0.78 / +0.01. Means +1.63 / +2.99 / +0.80 / -0.41 / +9.77 / +0.58. q0.5 means -0.15 / -0.17 / -0.04 / -0.33 / -0.65 / -0.06.
- ncq's q0.9 seed range 0.12 to 1.19 days; its censored all-row q0.9 loss (SF 2021 330.5, SF 2022 248.8, Seattle 2021 383.8, Seattle 2022 289.8) sits between nc3's and nc5's.
- The ncq thresholds: Austin 1,138 to 1,139 days (3.1 years), SF 1,203 to 1,240 (3.3 to 3.4), Seattle 2,309 to 2,391 (6.3 to 6.5). With SF held out the rule cuts Austin at 3.1 years and Seattle at 6.5 (20,357 to 20,538 open training rows, between nc3's 13,855 and nc5's 31,333); with Seattle held out it cuts both sources at about 3.2 years, close to nc3.
- (S1) Held: nc8 is 10.2 days above nc5 (band: > 5).
- (S2) Held, narrowly: nc2's SF mean +2.37 against nc5's +2.78 (0.41 lower, inside one seed range).
- (S3) Refuted: Seattle's 0.99 quantile is 6.3 to 6.5 years, outside 2 to 5; and ncq's mean +0.58 is 0.99 above nc5's (band 0.5).
- (S4) Held: the best fixed threshold is 3 years in both SF cells and 5 years in both Seattle cells (as session 92's S1).
- Close-out status (`results/closeout_status.json`, issued and no completion date, queried 2026-11-15): SF 484,215; since 2010, 84 to 92% of every issue year still reads "issued" (the 229,398 "expired" are mostly older). Seattle 33,077: 41% "expired", 31% "completed" with no completion date (56 to 84% of each issue year 2010 to 2015; 2 to 13% since 2016), 25% "issued" (up to 22% of an issue year to 2023). Austin 199,042: 58% "expired", 26% "void", 14% "active" (0 to 2% of each issue year to 2021, 7% of 2023, 15% of 2024, 92% of 2026). Among completed permits the status reads "complete"/"completed"/"final" for 92 to 99%.
- What this says (post hoc): the per-source rule gets the direction right (Seattle's real builds run longer than SF's or Austin's, so its cut should be later) but its 99th percentile puts Seattle's cut where Seattle's never-closed records still sit, and SF held out pays (+0.82 against nc3's +0.48); with Seattle held out it behaves like nc3 and loses Seattle's 5-year gain. So a cut set from the sources alone cannot know what the target needs: SF as a target wants SF-like short tails, Seattle wants the long ones. The status field is the cleaner signal where it exists. Austin and Seattle mark dead permits, and Seattle holds about 10,000 finished builds whose completion date is missing. SF's feed does not tell an abandoned permit from a running one. But a status is the status at the pull, not on D, so training on it leaks the future unless the feed keeps a status history; it is usable on permits old enough that their status on D would already have been settled.
- Limits: cascade2 only; 3 seeds; SF and Seattle held out only; one quantile (0.99) for ncq, not swept; no Austin cells for ncq; ncq was run before session 92's selection-set code existed, so it has no sel_closs.
Next: as session 92: a backtest at D - 2 years to choose the cut; and a cure-fraction weight per source city-class, fitted on Austin's and Seattle's permits whose status has settled (issued at least 3 years before D: expired/void = never closes, completed/final = closes), applied to SF by class. Check whether Seattle's "completed" permits with no completion date can be dated from another field and returned as completed rows.

## Session 92, 2026-11-15: the staleness threshold matters by city (3 years best in San Francisco, 5 in Seattle; 8 years costs 19 to 22 days in SF), but choosing it by the censored loss on the sources' Y-3 permits as seen on 1 January picks 8 years in all 6 city-years, mean regret 11.3 days in SF and Seattle and 60 to 68 in Austin, because that score falls with every open permit a model keeps; S1, S2, S5 held, S3 and S4 refuted

Why: session 91's 5-year staleness rule made the deployable borrowed model match the complete-label model on average (q0.9 -0.41 days), but it cost SF 1.6 to 2.9 days against cpin_es3 while it helped Seattle 5.7 to 6.9; the threshold was fixed before the run, never tuned. Its "Next" asked to sweep 2, 3, 5 and 8 years as a deployable choice.
- Method: variants `cpin_es3_nc2`, `_nc3`, `_nc5`, `_nc8` (`src/rounds.py`, `STALE`: 730, 1,095, 1,826, 2,922 days), each session 91's cpin_es3_nc with every source permit open at D for longer than the threshold dropped from training and from the Y-3 early-stopping set. New: each model also predicts a selection set a deployment has on D and that is the same for every threshold: all the sources' Y-3 permits, never-completed ones included and none dropped, open ones with their window at D, scored by the censored pinball loss (`sel_closs`). The selector takes, per held city-year, the threshold with the lowest q0.9 `sel_closs`. Test scoring as session 91 (completed test rows against session 88's `full`; censored loss over all test rows).
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull 2026-11-15; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --variants cpin_es3_nc2 cpin_es3_nc3 cpin_es3_nc5 cpin_es3_nc8 --held sf|seattle --out-name rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`, summarised by `--summarise-sweep`. The selection set overlaps the early-stopping set (both are Y-3), so the selector is not out of sample for the k >= 3 thresholds, whose early-stopping set is nearly all of it; disclosed, not fixed. Austin held out was added after launch, on the spare core (1 thread), reported but not tested.
Predictions (written before any run; q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball-loss change against full on completed test-year rows, 3 seeds):
- (S1) The best threshold differs by city: in SF nc3 beats nc5 in both years; in Seattle nc5 or nc8 beats nc3 in both years.
- (S2) nc8's SF and Seattle q0.9 mean change is more than 1 day above nc5's (older never-closed records hurt).
- (S3) The Y-3 selector's mean q0.9 regret against the per-cell best threshold is at most 1 day.
- (S4) The Y-3 selector's SF and Seattle q0.9 mean change is at most 0 (it matches or beats the complete-label model).
- (S5, check) nc5's q0.9 test loss is within 0.3 days of session 91's in all 4 cells.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Fresh pull 2026-11-15 19:40 to 19:51 UTC, same row counts as session 91 (SF 1,298,381, Seattle 194,311, Austin 2,385,435). Runs 19:52 to about 22:10 UTC on `/usr/bin/python3.13`, SF and Seattle side by side at `CPIN_THREADS=2`, Austin after at 1 thread. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_sweep_{sf,seattle,austin}_i2c.json` (one per process), merged into `results/rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`, summarised in `results/rounds_score_sweep_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-sweep results/rounds_score_sweep_i2c.json`, SF and Seattle only; Austin's cells by `sweep_summary(..., held=("austin",))`, numbers below).
- (S5, check) Held: nc5 reproduces session 91's q0.9 test loss to every printed digit in all 4 cells (SF 2021 +2.46, Seattle 2021 -3.95 against full).
- What the thresholds keep: 2 and 3 years train on the same rows (every source permit issued in Y-4 or earlier and open at D is past 3 years, and Y-2's are under 2), 13,855 to 32,282 open training rows; they differ only in the early-stopping set, which at 2 years loses all of Y-3's open permits (10,228 to 26,237) and at 3 years keeps them. 5 years keeps 31,333 to 76,709 open training rows, 8 years 51,330 to 133,454.
- cascade2's q0.9 test-loss change against full, completed test rows, days (nc2 / nc3 / nc5 / nc8): SF 2021 +3.54 / +0.90 / +2.46 / +19.09. SF 2022 +1.19 / +0.05 / +3.10 / +21.73. Seattle 2021 +5.53 / +1.50 / -3.95 / -0.98. Seattle 2022 +1.68 / +0.73 / -3.22 / -0.78. Means +2.99 / +0.80 / -0.41 / +9.77. q0.5 means -0.17 / -0.04 / -0.33 / -0.65.
- Austin held out (reported, not tested): q0.9 2021 -4.29 / -2.34 / +14.84 / +55.77, 2022 -8.42 / -5.64 / +10.43 / +59.83; q0.5 2021 +3.31 / +14.14 / +53.49 / +155.05, 2022 -3.37 / +1.89 / +43.06 / +99.20. Austin's builds are shorter than its sources' (session 90), and every never-closed SF or Seattle record kept lengthens its borrowed tail.
- q0.9 rounds, mean of 3 seeds, SF/Seattle/Austin: nc2 8 to 18, nc3 21 to 178, nc5 4 to 119, nc8 4 to 17. With Y-3's open permits in early stopping (nc3) the SF and Seattle tail models run 141 to 178 rounds; without them (nc2) 10 to 18, as session 87 found for es3.
- Selection score (q0.9 censored loss on all source Y-3 permits as seen on D, same rows for every threshold): it falls with the threshold in all 6 cells, strictly at every step; nc8's is 0.82 of nc2's in SF, 0.57 to 0.59 in Seattle and 0.37 in Austin. So the selector picks nc8 everywhere.
- (S1) Held: in SF nc3 beats nc5 in both years (by 1.56 and 3.05 days); in Seattle nc5 beats nc3 in both (by 5.45 and 3.95).
- (S2) Held: nc8's mean q0.9 change is 10.18 days above nc5's (band: more than 1).
- (S3) Refuted: the selector's mean q0.9 regret against the per-cell best threshold is 11.32 days (18.19 and 21.67 in SF, 2.98 and 2.45 in Seattle; band 1). Austin: 60.06 and 68.24.
- (S4) Refuted: the selector's SF and Seattle mean q0.9 change is +9.77 days (band: at most 0).
- Post hoc, not tested: the best fixed threshold on SF and Seattle is 5 years (-0.41, session 91's choice); over all 6 cells with Austin it is 3 years (mean -0.80, worst cell +1.50) against 2 years (-0.13, worst +5.53), 5 years (+3.94, worst +14.84) and 8 years (+25.78, worst +59.83). But 3 years costs Austin 14.1 days at q0.5 in 2021, and its q0.9 seed range is 4.73 and 9.75 days in Austin (0.26 to 0.82 in SF and Seattle), so Austin's order between 2 and 3 years is inside seed noise. In SF and Seattle the q0.9 seed ranges of nc2 to nc5 are 0.26 to 1.94 days, below S1's gaps except SF 2021 (1.56 against nc5's 1.30); nc8's are 3.3 to 15.3.
- What this says (post hoc): the same mislabel that broke session 91's nc variant breaks the selector. A never-closed permit on D is read as "at least its window", so on the Y-3 selection set any model that predicts longer is charged less, and the threshold that keeps the most never-closed records wins the score while losing the test. A censored score is a valid selection rule only where "open" means "still running"; in these feeds about a quarter to two-fifths of "open" means "not recorded". A deployable selector needs either a score that cannot be lowered by predicting long for unrecorded permits (completed-by-D pinball on Y-3, which is biased short instead, or a backtest on an older decision date whose open permits have since been closed or gone stale), or a model of which open permits are real (the cure-fraction model session 91 proposed).
- Limits: cascade2 only; 3 seeds; one selection score, pre-registered, and its alternative (completed-by-D pinball on Y-3) not computed, because the run did not save Y-3 predictions; nc2 and nc3 differ only in early stopping, so the sweep's low end tests the early-stopping set as much as the training rows; Austin run at 1 thread (LightGBM sums can differ in the last digits from 2 threads); no intervals beyond the seed ranges.
Next: a deployable selector that is not fooled by never-closed records: (a) a backtest at D - 2 years (train on years to Y-6, score on Y-2's permits as known at the pull, mature enough that open now means unrecorded), choosing the threshold there; (b) the cure-fraction weight per source city-class from status fields (SF "issued"/"expired", Seattle and Austin to check). Save the selection-set predictions so other scores can be computed after the run.

## Session 91, 2026-11-15: putting the permits never completed by the pull back as open rows wrecks the borrowed tail model (q0.9 +181 days against the complete-label model in SF and Seattle; Seattle +311 and +344), because 23 to 39% of source permits are never closed out and in SF most still read "issued" years later; dropping any permit open more than 5 years on the decision date turns that into the first deployable variant to match the complete-label model (q0.9 -0.41 days on average, cpin_es3 +1.63) and the best on the censored score over all test permits in 4 of 4; N1, N3, N5 held, N2 and N4 refuted

Why: session 90's censored pinball loss closed two-thirds of the deployable model's tail gap, but its frame still dropped every permit never completed by the pull, so "open at D" meant "open at D and completed later": the model was told, in hindsight, which open permits would finish. Its "Next" asked to put them back as open rows, since a deployment on D sees them open too.
- Method: `boosted.load_frame(keep_open=True)` keeps rows with an issue date and no completion date, labelled y = +inf; `cpin.censor` makes each an open row with window c = D - issue day. Variants (`src/rounds.py`): `cpin_es3` (session 90, rerun on this pull as the comparator), `cpin_es3_nc` (the never-completed source permits added as open rows to training and to the Y-3 early-stopping set), `cpin_es3_nc5` (as nc, with a staleness rule a deployment can apply: any source permit open at D for more than 5 years (1,826 days) is dropped as presumed abandoned). The test year is scored as before on its completed rows (pinball, against session 88's `full`), and also by a censored test loss over all its rows, its never-completed permits entering with their window at the pull date.
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull 2026-11-15; `rounds.py DIR --score --keep-open --variants cpin_es3 cpin_es3_nc cpin_es3_nc5 --held sf seattle --out-name rounds_score_nc_i2c.json`; Austin held out after if time allows (reported, not tested).

Austin was not run (time).
- What this says (post hoc): a deployment on D cannot tell an open permit that will finish from one that never will, and in these feeds the second kind is common: most of SF's and Seattle's never-completed permits are records nobody closed, and the censored loss reads each as "at least this long", so whole classes (SF alteration as a source for Seattle) lift their tail to the windows of permits issued up to a decade earlier. The loss is right about the label it is told; the label is wrong, because "not completed" in these feeds mostly means "not recorded". A staleness rule, which needs nothing a deployment lacks, removes the oldest of them and keeps the recent ones, which still carry tail information: in Seattle the borrowed model with them beats the complete-label model by 3 to 4 days at q0.9. In SF it costs 1.6 to 2.9 days against cpin_es3, so the rule's 5 years is not right for every source mix; the threshold was fixed before the run and not tuned.
- Limits: cascade2 only; 3 seeds; one staleness threshold, not swept; no Austin cells; no q0.1; no interval on the loss changes (per-seed losses are now kept: cpin_es3_nc5's q0.9 seed range is 0.5 to 2.0 days, smaller than its Seattle gains and of the size of its SF costs); the censored all-row score treats a never-completed test permit as a lower bound at the pull date, which is generous to models that predict long for permits that were simply never closed, so it cannot by itself say which model is better; status is used only descriptively (SF), never as a feature.
Next: treat completion as a competing risk. Fit P(a permit is ever closed out) per source city-class from the status field where the feed has one (SF "expired", "cancelled"; Seattle and Austin fields to check), and weight each open row by the chance it is a real build still running (a cure-fraction model) instead of a fixed staleness cut; sweep the staleness threshold (2, 3, 5, 8 years) on Y-3 as a deployable choice; run Austin.

## Session 90, 2026-11-15: a censored pinball objective for LightGBM lets the deployable borrowed model use the open permits: q0.9 +1.63 days against the complete-label model in SF and Seattle (IPCW +2.53, unweighted +4.74; better than IPCW in 4 of 4), and with complete-label early stopping it recovers 47 to 89% of what dropping them costs; 6 of 7 predictions held, P3 refuted (q0.9 predictions above 3,650 days in 3 of 24 cell-seeds, on at most 3 test rows)

Why: session 89 found Portnoy pseudo-rows wreck LightGBM's quantile tail because leaf renewal snaps leaves onto the pseudo-rows' far-right labels. Its "Next" asked for a loss that charges an open permit only while the prediction is below its window and never sets a leaf from a stand-in label.
- Method (`src/cpin.py`): an open source permit (issued before D = 1 January Y, not completed by D) enters with its window c = D - issue day and loss tau * max(c - q, 0); completed rows keep the pinball loss. Each round grows a LightGBM tree on this loss's gradient (custom objective, unit hessian) and then sets every leaf to the shrunken minimiser of the censored loss over its rows: a weighted percentile of the residuals (weight 1 per completed row, tau per open one, level tau * n). This is the same leaf renewal LightGBM's quantile objective does, with the censored loss in place of the pinball loss; with no open rows it reduces to it (`tests/test_cpin.py`). Departure from session 89's "Next": leaves are renewed from the censored loss rather than given a fixed hessian scale, because the scale had no source and renewal matches the built-in objective the baselines use.
- Variants (`src/rounds.py`): `cpin_cut` (cut_only's years with the open permits of years <= Y-2 kept as lower bounds; early-stopped on the whole Y-1 with complete labels), `cpin_es3` (es3's years with open permits in training and in the Y-3 early-stopping set, scored by the censored loss: deployable), `cpin_es3_done` (es3's completed rows through the custom loop: checks the loop against es3). full, cut_only, es3, ipcw_es3 come from session 88's `rounds_score_ipcw_i2c.json`.
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class; H = sf, seattle (Austin held out if time allows, reported, not tested); Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull; `rounds.py DIR --score --variants cpin_es3 cpin_cut --out-name rounds_score_cpin_i2c.json`, then `cpin_es3_done` on SF 2021.
Predictions (written before any run; q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss on all test-year rows, 3 seeds, change against session 88's full):

- (P2) cpin_es3's q0.9 loss is below ipcw_es3's in at least 3 of the 4 SF and Seattle city-years.
- (P3) No cpin_es3 or cpin_cut q0.9 test prediction exceeds 3,650 days in any cell and seed.
- (P4) cpin_cut's q0.9 loss is within 1 day of full's in at least 3 of the 4 (G1 and C1 again).
- (P5) cpin_cut recovers at least half of cut_only's q0.9 cost in at least 3 of the 4.
- (P6) cpin_es3's SF and Seattle q0.5 mean change is at most ipcw_es3's +0.34 days.
- (P7, check) cpin_es3_done's SF 2021 q0.9 test loss is within 0.5 days of es3's.
Refuted if any part fails.

Sessions 87 to 89 found the borrowed tail too long for Austin, so the models that lose the long source builds win there; the censored loss gives those builds back, and Austin's gain shrinks by 2.4 to 4.9 days against ipcw_es3. No Austin q0.9 prediction exceeds 2,293 days. The direction is the same as in SF and Seattle (the open permits lengthen the tail); whether that helps depends on whether the held-out city's builds are as long as the sources'.
- What this says (post hoc): the open permits carry real information about the tail, and a boosted quantile model can use it once their only claim is "at least this long". The loss's minimiser in a leaf is a percentile in which an open row has weight tau and sits at its window, so it can lift a leaf only as far as the windows the leaf holds; the tree's split search still sees every open row whose window is above the current prediction. The deployable gap to the complete-label model shrinks from +4.74 days (es3) to +1.63, two-thirds of it closed, without any future label. What remains comes from two places: the loss charges an open row nothing once the prediction passes its window, so the model never learns how far past the window the build ran, and the early-stopping year Y-3 is itself censored.
- Limits: cascade2 only; 3 seeds; no interval on the loss changes (per-row losses and per-seed losses not kept; the q0.9 gaps between cpin_es3 and ipcw_es3 are 0.72 to 1.22 days, and the evidence is that all 4 go the same way, not any single cell); no q0.1; the frame still drops permits never completed by the pull (as sessions 88 and 89), so "open at D" means open at D and completed later, and a deployment would also see the permits that never complete; the loss is not a proper scoring rule for the censored rows.
Next: Austin's sign and the never-completed permits. Put the permits never completed by the pull back into the frame as open rows (they are lower bounds too, and a real deployment sees them); then re-run the select_deploy pipeline (ipcw3 class selection, gate) with cpin_es3 as the transfer model, against session 87's end-to-end losses.

## Session 89, 2026-11-14: keeping the open permits as lower bounds (Portnoy-style censored pseudo-rows, Kaplan-Meier levels) makes the borrowed tail model far worse, not better: +36.4 days at q0.9 in SF and Seattle (cut variant) and +84.9 (deployable), against ipcw_es3's +2.53, because LightGBM's quantile leaf renewal snaps leaves onto the pseudo-rows' far-right labels; capping them at the longest completed build halves the damage (+21.8, +14.5) but q0.9 predictions still reach 25,600 days (C1 to C5 and K1 to K6 refuted)

Why: session 88 found 55 to 65% of the source permits the training cut drops (still open on D = 1 January Y) outlast every window their city-year has, so no reweighting of completed rows can stand for them. Its "Next" asked to keep each open permit as a lower bound on its build time.
- Method (Portnoy 2003's redistribution of censored mass, with the local quantile level taken from a stratified Kaplan-Meier): each source permit issued in years <= Y-2 and still open at D has window c = D - issue day and is right-censored at c. Its quantile level tau_i = F(c) comes from a Kaplan-Meier fit over the same source city and cascade2 class (all of its rows issued <= Y-2, as seen at D: events at y for completed, censored at c for open). For a model of quantile tau: if tau <= tau_i the permit is one pseudo-row at "+infinity" (y = 100,000 days) with weight 1; otherwise two pseudo-rows, at c with weight (tau - tau_i)/(1 - tau_i) and at +infinity with weight (1 - tau)/(1 - tau_i). LightGBM's quantile objective only uses the sign of the residual and renews leaves by weighted percentile, so the +infinity value only needs to exceed every prediction. Uses only what is known at D. The frame drops permits never completed by the pull (as session 88's G), so "open at D" here means open at D and completed later.
- Variants: `cens_cut` (cut_only's rows plus the open permits of years <= Y-2 as pseudo-rows; early-stopped on the whole Y-1 unweighted: does keeping the open permits give back what the cut takes?) and `cens_es3` (es3's rows plus the open permits of years <= Y-4 and Y-2 in training and of Y-3 in early stopping, both augmented: the deployable version). full, cut_only, es3, ipcw_cut and ipcw_es3 come from session 88's `rounds_score_ipcw_i2c.json`; `full` is refitted on SF 2021 to check the fresh pull reproduces it.
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class; H = austin, sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull; `rounds.py DIR --score --variants cens_cut cens_es3 --out-name rounds_score_cens_i2c.json`.
Predictions (written before any run; q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss on all test-year rows, 3 seeds, change against session 88's full):
- (C1) cens_cut's q0.9 loss is within 1 day of full's in at least 3 of the 4 SF and Seattle city-years (session 88's G1 again).
- (C2) cens_cut recovers at least half of cut_only's q0.9 cost in at least 3 of those 4 (ipcw_cut: 18 to 33%).
- (C3) cens_es3's q0.9 loss is below ipcw_es3's in at least 3 of those 4, and its SF and Seattle mean change is below ipcw_es3's +2.53 days.
- (C4) Austin: cens_cut's q0.9 change is above cut_only's in both years (the long builds Austin gained from losing come back).
- (C5) cens_cut's q0.5 loss is within 0.5 days of full's in at least 5 of 6 city-years.
Refuted if any part fails.

Post hoc variant (designed after the first five cells, before the rest of the run finished and before fitting it): Austin 2021 and 2022 cens_es3 came out +100.7 and +96.1 days at q0.9 and SF 2021 cens_cut +16.8 with 1 to 2 rounds. The design's claim that the +infinity label "only needs to exceed every prediction" is wrong for trees: LightGBM's quantile objective renews each leaf to the weighted percentile of its residuals, so a leaf in which open permits above tau_i hold more than 1 - tau of the weight is set to about 100,000 days (times the learning rate). `cens_cut_cap` and `cens_es3_cap` (`censored_pseudo(cap=True)`) put the upper pseudo-row at max(c, the longest build completed by D in the same source city and class) instead: Efron's convention of leaving the mass past the last observation on it. Every run now also saves the q0.9 test predictions' maximum and share above 3,650 days. Predictions for the capped variants: (K1 to K5) C1 to C5 with cens_*_cap in place of cens_*; (K6) no capped q0.9 test prediction exceeds 3,650 days in any cell and seed.

Outcome. Fresh pull 2026-11-14 23:08 to 23:12 UTC: SF 1,298,381 and Seattle 194,285 rows (as session 88), Austin 2,385,402 (+142). `full` refitted on SF 2021 reproduces session 88's test losses and row counts exactly (`results/rounds_score_cens_repro_i2c.json`). One process, `/usr/bin/python3.13`: the pre-registered variants 23:13 to 00:37 UTC, the capped ones 00:37 to 01:51. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_cens_i2c.json`, `results/rounds_score_cens_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-cens ...`), `results/cens_reach_i2c.json` (`src/cens_reach.py`, no model).
- What the pseudo-rows hold (`cens_reach`): open permits are 0.45 to 0.56% of the source rows of years <= Y-2 for SF and Seattle held out (0.97 to 1.28% for Austin). Their Kaplan-Meier level tau_i is at least 0.9 for 90 to 95% of SF's and Austin's open permits and 35 to 37% of Seattle's, so at q0.9 most of them are pure "longer than the prediction" rows. No city-class stratum with 1,000 or more rows has more than 2.7% of its q0.9 weight on them, so q0.9 is identified at stratum level; the failure is inside leaves. The longest completed build per stratum is 4,644 to 22,016 days (12 to 60 years: record errors, almost certainly), against q0.99 values of 905 to 3,987.
- cascade2's q0.9 test-loss change against full, days (cut_only / ipcw_cut / cens_cut / cens_cut_cap ; es3 / ipcw_es3 / cens_es3 / cens_es3_cap): SF 2021 +1.45 / +1.19 / +16.77 / +10.66 ; +3.28 / +1.77 / +8.94 / +1.69. SF 2022 +1.03 / +0.69 / +11.46 / +6.40 ; +1.63 / +0.99 / +43.82 / +6.18. Seattle 2021 +5.14 / +4.06 / +64.08 / +40.61 ; +10.28 / +4.16 / +194.94 / +31.91. Seattle 2022 +3.49 / +2.52 / +53.30 / +29.57 ; +3.78 / +3.20 / +92.03 / +18.32. SF and Seattle mean: cens_cut +36.40, cens_cut_cap +21.81, cens_es3 +84.93, cens_es3_cap +14.52 (ipcw_es3 +2.53). Austin 2021 / 2022: cens_cut -11.31 / -3.25, cens_cut_cap -5.12 / -5.17, cens_es3 +100.71 / +96.07, cens_es3_cap +15.83 / +3.43.
- Rounds: cens_cut's q0.9 model early-stops after 1 or 2 rounds in every SF and Seattle cell (1 to 9 for Austin), cens_cut_cap after 3 to 4: the first rounds already hurt the complete-label Y-1 set. Early-stopped on augmented Y-3 rows, cens_es3 runs 24 to 299 rounds and is the worst variant.
- q0.9 test predictions (capped variants only; the +infinity run did not save them): maxima 515 to 10,784 days for cens_cut_cap and 12,900 to 25,600 for cens_es3_cap; above 3,650 days in 33 of 36 cell-seeds, on up to 3.95% of test rows.
- q0.5: cens_cut is within 0.5 days of full in 2 of 6 city-years (SF 2021 -0.02, SF 2022 +0.28); the capped variant's q0.5 is the same in every cell but Austin 2021 (-1.98 against -1.87): at the median the upper pseudo-row sits above the prediction either way.
- (C1) Refuted: cens_cut's q0.9 within 1 day of full's in 0 of 4. (C2) Refuted: it recovers none of cut_only's cost; it adds 10 to 14 times that cost on top. (C3) Refuted: cens_es3 below ipcw_es3 in 0 of 4; mean +84.93 against +2.53. (C4) Refuted: Austin's cens_cut change is above cut_only's in 2022 (-3.25 against -5.67) but below in 2021 (-11.31 against -2.34). (C5) Refuted: 2 of 6.
- (K1 to K5) Refuted for the capped variants: 0 of 4, 0 of 4, 1 of 4 (SF 2021, +1.69 against +1.77; mean +14.52 against +2.53), 1 of 2, 2 of 6. (K6) Refuted: q0.9 predictions above 3,650 days in 33 of 36 cell-seeds.
- What this says (post hoc): Portnoy's redistribution works for linear quantile regression because the fit only sees the sign of each residual, so a pseudo-row at "+infinity" only needs to be above the fitted line. Gradient-boosted quantile trees also use the residual's sign to grow a tree, but LightGBM then sets each leaf to the weighted percentile of its residuals (leaf renewal). In a leaf where open permits above their tau_i carry more than 1 - tau of the weight, that percentile is one of the far-right labels, and the leaf moves by up to 0.15 times 100,000 days in one round (inferred from the 1-to-2-round stops and the capped run's prediction maxima; leaf values were not logged). Capping the label at the stratum's longest completed build only moves the far-right value to 12 to 60 years, which this data has because of record errors, and additive trees still stack leaves past any one label. The open permits can enter a boosted quantile model as lower bounds only through a loss that charges them when the prediction is below their window and never sets a leaf from their position: a custom censored-pinball objective with no leaf renewal, or the IPCW weights that already work (ipcw_es3).
- Limits: cascade2 only; 3 seeds; no interval on the loss changes (per-row losses not kept); the capped variant was designed after seeing five pre-registered cells; the KM levels are per source city and class, not conditional on cost or description, so a leaf can hold permits whose real level differs; open permits never completed by the pull are not in the frame (as session 88's G); the +infinity run did not save prediction maxima.
Next: a custom LightGBM objective for censored pinball loss (open row: gradient -tau while the prediction is below its window, 0 above; completed rows: the usual pinball gradient), with a constant hessian scaled so a round moves about as far as the built-in objective's renewed leaves, early-stopped on es3's Y-3 rows scored by the same censored loss; pre-register "SF and Seattle q0.9 mean below ipcw_es3's +2.53 days and no prediction above 3,650 days".

## Session 88, 2026-11-14: inverse-probability-of-censoring weights halve the deployable borrowed model's tail cost in SF and Seattle (q0.9 +2.53 days against the complete-label model, es3 +4.74; better in 4 of 4) but give back only 18 to 33% of what the training cut takes, because 55 to 65% of the dropped permits outlast every window their city-year has (G3 held; G1, G2, G4, G5 refuted)

Why: session 87 found the largest deployable cost in SF and Seattle is the 0.45 to 0.56% of source training permits still open on 1 January Y (D), the longest builds: dropping them alone costs 1.0 to 5.1 days at q0.9. Its "Next" asked to keep their weight without their labels.
- New: `transfer.ipcw_completed(d, D, floor)` keeps the rows completed by D and weights each by 1/G(y), G(t) = share of the same source city-year's permits issued at least t days before D (each city-year's issue days are known in full at D), floored at 0.05 (weight <= 20, select_deploy's floor). `boosted.fit_predict(w=, wca=)` passes weights to LightGBM. `src/rounds.py` gains two variants: `ipcw_cut` (cut_only's rows, IPCW-weighted, early-stopped on the whole Y-1 unweighted: isolates whether the weights give back what the cut takes) and `ipcw_es3` (es3's rows, training and early-stopping rows both weighted: deployable), plus `--floor` and `--out-name`. 3 new tests (weights against a hand-worked case; the ipcw variants fit on exactly cut_only's / es3's rows and reduce to them with floor 1).
Design (fixed before any run): cascade2 class only; H = austin, sf, seattle; Y = 2021, 2022; 3 seeds; fresh pull; `rounds.py DIR --score --variants full cut_only es3 ipcw_cut ipcw_es3 --out-name rounds_score_ipcw_i2c.json`. Sensitivity (reported, not tested): the same two ipcw variants with `--floor 0.2` (weight <= 5).
Predictions (written before any run; q0.9 = cascade2's mean pinball loss on all test-year rows, 3 seeds):
- (G1) ipcw_cut's q0.9 loss is within 1 day of full's in at least 3 of the 4 SF and Seattle city-years (session 87's "Next").
- (G2) ipcw_cut recovers at least half of cut_only's q0.9 cost against full in at least 3 of those 4.
- (G3) ipcw_es3's q0.9 loss is below es3's in at least 3 of those 4.
- (G4) Austin: ipcw_cut's q0.9 loss is above cut_only's in both years (the weights give back the long builds Austin gained from losing).
- (G5) ipcw_cut's q0.5 loss is within 0.5 days of full's in all 6 city-years.
Refuted if any part fails.

Outcome. Fresh pull 2026-11-14 14:08 to 14:14 UTC: SF 1,298,381 (+95 on session 87), Austin 2,385,260, Seattle 194,285. Outputs: `results/rounds_score_ipcw_i2c.json`, `results/rounds_score_ipcw_i2c_summary.json` (`rounds.py x --summarise-ipcw ...`). `full` and `cut_only` reproduce session 87's `rounds_score_i2c.json` exactly (largest test-loss difference 0.000 days, same training row counts), so the extra SF rows change nothing here.
- The weights: 0.23 to 0.30% of the kept training rows get weight above 1 in SF and Seattle (0.39 to 0.57% for Austin held out; max 20); total weight is 0.15 to 0.19% above the row count (0.25 to 0.38%).

SF and Seattle mean: cut_only +2.78, ipcw_cut +2.12, es3 +4.74, ipcw_es3 +2.53.
- q0.5, same order: SF and Seattle mean +0.70 / +0.55 / +0.48 / +0.34; Austin 2021 -1.13 / +0.55 / -0.50 / +0.30, Austin 2022 +2.06 / +0.50 / -1.98 / -2.63.
- q0.9 rounds, mean of 3 seeds (full / es3 / ipcw_es3): SF 2021 121 / 14 / 22, SF 2022 88 / 17 / 20, Seattle 2021 50 / 14 / 121, Seattle 2022 84 / 148 / 144, Austin 34 / 8 / 15 and 44 / 9 / 10.
- (G1) Refuted: ipcw_cut's q0.9 is within 1 day of full's in 1 of 4 (SF 2022, +0.69), not 3.
- (G2) Refuted: ipcw_cut recovers 18% (SF 2021), 33% (SF 2022), 21% (Seattle 2021) and 28% (Seattle 2022) of cut_only's q0.9 cost, never half.
- (G3) Held: ipcw_es3's q0.9 is below es3's in 4 of 4, by 1.51, 0.64, 6.12 and 0.58 days.
- (G4) Refuted: in Austin, ipcw_cut's q0.9 is above cut_only's in 2021 (-2.11 against -2.34) but below in 2022 (-7.94 against -5.67).
- (G5) Refuted: ipcw_cut's q0.5 is within 0.5 days of full's in 3 of 6 (Seattle +0.94 and +1.15, Austin 2021 +0.55 are not).
Diagnostic (designed after seeing SF 2021's ipcw_cut cell, before the other cells; no model fitted; `src/ipcw_reach.py`, `results/ipcw_reach_i2c.json`): of the source training rows the cut drops (0.45 to 0.56% in SF and Seattle, 0.97 to 1.28% for Austin held out), 55 to 65% have a build time longer than the longest window any permit of their city-year has at D, so G = 0 for them and no reweighting of completed rows can stand in for them. The weights add back 29 to 39% of the dropped row count, and move the training q0.99 from the kept rows' 1,156 to 1,357 days to 1,166 to 1,381, against 1,333 to 1,639 for all rows: under a tenth of the gap.
- What this says (post hoc): IPCW is the right correction for the part of the cut that is random censoring, and it helps where the model early-stops (ipcw_es3, where weighting Y-3's completed rows also lets Seattle 2021's tail model run 121 rounds instead of 14). But most of the cut is not reweightable: a permit issued in year Y-2 that is still open two years later has no completed peer with that build time in its city-year. Recovering it needs a model of the tail beyond the window (a parametric or survival-forest tail, or a censored quantile loss that uses the open permits' lower bound), not weights.
- Limits: the floor-0.2 sensitivity runs set in the design were not run (time: one 2.75-hour serial run); ipcw_es3 weights both the training and early-stopping rows, so the two effects are not separated; one class candidate (cascade2), no interval on the loss changes (per-row losses not kept); 3 cities, 2 test years, 3 seeds. G is estimated from the issue days of the permits the pull lists as completed (the frame drops never-completed permits), as in select_deploy.
Next: a censored quantile loss for the source rows still open at D: keep each open permit as a lower bound (its build time is at least its window) in a Portnoy/Peng-Huang style censored quantile fit, or LightGBM with a custom objective that charges an open row only when the prediction falls below its window; pre-register "q0.9 within 1 day of full in 3 of 4 SF and Seattle city-years" again, plus ipcw_es3's +2.53-day mean as the bar to beat.
